    `||F(T)||>=cT^(alpha/2)`, contradicting normalization's
    `||F||=o(T^(alpha/2))`.

    Hence two independent channels, arbitrary constant cross-channel
    mixing, fixed mixtures, persistent regular rotation, and every acute-cone
    path with bounded logarithmic derivative fail.  The sole formal escape
    requires `||TB_T||/||B||` to be unbounded along a sequence, i.e. violent
    phase-coordinate oscillation rather than a regular two-channel
    cancellation.

120. **The exact past-cone differential inclusion has only the trivial
    normalized primitive, without any rotation regularity.**  Continue the
    notation of item 119 after conjugating `C` to the identity, assume `F=TB`
    is absolutely continuous with `F(0)=0`, put `p=F'`, and set
    `W=(p-alpha F/T)/(1-alpha)`.  Let
    `P_T=closure cone{p(S):0<S<=T}`.  Bochner integration is a limit of
    positive conic sums, so `F(T)=int_0^T p(S)dS` belongs to `P_T` exactly.

    If the separated-tail sign were strong enough to place
    `W(T) in P_T^*`, then `F(T) dot W(T)>=0`.  Hence
    `F dot p>=alpha||F||^2/T` and, for `Y=||F||^2`,
    `Y'>=2alpha Y/T` almost everywhere.  Once `Y(T_0)>0`, integration gives
    `||F(T)||>=cT^alpha`, contradicting the exact-normalization requirement
    `||F(T)||=o(T^(alpha/2))`.  If `F` is never nonzero, the feature field is
    trivial.  This proof works in every finite Hilbert multiplicity and
    permits unbounded logarithmic derivatives, arbitrarily fast rotations,
    and arbitrary absolutely continuous amplitude oscillations.

    The hypothesis is slightly stronger than the sign directly obtained in
    item 119.  The physical majorant tests coefficients at sufficiently
    separated earlier points, but not those lying in the shrinking physical
    window `0<x-y<D` where `q(x-y)<0`; in phase coordinates that excluded
    window may itself expand.  Thus the sole remaining primitive loophole is
    to concentrate the conic contribution carrying `F(T)` inside this moving
    recent window, so that `F(T)` is not in the cone tested against `W(T)`.
    This is a precise nonuniform phase-boundary mechanism, not ordinary
    multichannel or violent-rotation cancellation.

121. **Fixed separated tests close every finite-channel common-phase
    primitive, including violent moving-window bursts.**  Retain item 120's
    notation in finite dimension and let
    `A(T)=||B(T)||+||TB_T(T)||`.  Operator-norm matrix Karamata gives, for
    each fixed phase `S`,
    `scaledGram(T,S)=W(T) dot p(S)+r_S(T)`, with
    `|r_S(T)|<=epsilon_S(T)A(T)` and `epsilon_S(T)->0`.  No bound on
    `||TB_T||/||B||` is imposed.

    First suppose `P=closure cone range(p)` is a pointed two-dimensional
    cone.  Choose two attained vectors `p_1,p_2` arbitrarily close to its
    extreme rays so that `Q=cone{p_1,p_2}` contains a vector
    `v in int(Q) intersect int(P^*)`; take `v` as a positive finite conic
    combination of the two tests.  Their two fixed-point majorant
    inequalities and the elementary Hoffman bound for two planar halfspaces
    put `W` within `delta(T)A(T)`, `delta(T)->0`, of `Q^*`.  If `z in Q^*`
    is a closest point, interiority gives `v dot z>=c||z||`.  Therefore
    `v dot W>=c||W||-Cdelta A`.  Since
    `TB_T=(1-alpha)(W-B)`,
    `A<=(2-alpha)||B||+(1-alpha)||W||`; absorbing the vanishing
    `delta||W||` term yields `v dot W>=-o(||B||)`, even during arbitrarily
    violent rotations.

    Now `B=F/T in P` and `v in int(P^*)`, so, for nonzero `F`,
    `y=F dot v>0` and `B dot v>=c_0||B||`.  The exact differential relation
    gives
    `y'=p dot v=alpha y/T+(1-alpha)W dot v`
    `>=(alpha-o(1))y/T`.  In particular, eventually
    `y'>=(alpha/2)y/T`, and hence `y>=cT^(alpha/2)`, contradicting
    `||F||=o(T^(alpha/2))`.  Choosing the fixed rays still closer gives an
    exponent arbitrarily near `alpha`; there is no boundary case.

    The finite-dimensional extension uses a proper-cone lemma, not an
    unproved planar analogy.  If a closed pointed cone `P` spans `E`, choose
    `ell in int_E(P^*)` and the compact base
    `B_0={u in P:ell(u)=1}`.  If `x_0` minimizes `||x||^2` on this base, its
    variational inequality gives
    `<x_0,u>>=||x_0||^2>0` for every `u in B_0`.  Adding a sufficiently small
    vector in `int_E(P)` produces
    `v in int_E(P) intersect int_E(P^*)`.  Compact unit sections then give
    `v dot u>=c||u||` on `P` and `v dot z>=c'||z||` on `P^*`.
    Approximate v and a finite epsilon-net of the compact base by finite
    conic combinations of attained `p(S_i)`.  Openness preserves both
    interior conditions, and the finite-dimensional polyhedral error bound
    gives `dist(W,P^*)<=C[max_i(-W dot p(S_i))_+ +epsilon||W||]`.
    The fixed-point Gram estimates, followed by the same absorption, prove
    `v dot W>=-o(||B||)` and hence the scalar growth contradiction above.

    If P has lineality, pass to `E/lin(P)`: fixed attained generators in both
    lineality directions force the corresponding W component to be
    `o(||B||)`, while the proper quotient supplies v.  If P is a subspace,
    those two-sided tests directly give `W=o(B)` and the radial ODE
    contradiction.  `P={0}` is trivial.  Thus the proof is valid in every
    fixed finite channel multiplicity.

    Thus every absolutely continuous finite-channel common-phase primitive
    satisfying the stated matrix-covariance asymptotics is zero.  Coefficient
    bursts confined to the moving near-diagonal window do not escape,
    because only fixed coefficient points, already far outside that window,
    enter the cone estimate.

122. **Every arbitrary positive-floor prime-shortcut metric fails, including
    nonattained floors.**  Let every undirected prime-power shortcut have an
    arbitrary state/channel cost `a_q(z)>=b>0`; no continuity, calibration,
    or attainment is assumed.  Put `X=e^x` and
    `d_P(x)=C(x)^(-1)sum_q c_q[K(x+logq)+K(x-logq)]`.

    The prime-degree tail has an exact zero-canceling Mellin expansion.  Set
    `w(r)=2r^(-1/2)K(log r)`.  In the repository normalization,
    `int_0^inf w(r)r^(s-1)dr=xi(s)`.  Thus, initially on `Re s>1`,
    `S_w(X)=sum_n Lambda(n)w(n/X)` equals
    `(2pi i)^(-1)int_((sigma))[-zeta'/zeta(s)]xi(s)X^sds`
    `=(2pi i)^(-1)int_((sigma))-G(s)zeta'(s)X^sds`, where
    `G(s)=(1/2)s(s-1)pi^(-s/2)Gamma(s/2)`.  The last integrand contains no
    reciprocal zeta factor.  Gamma decay and polynomial vertical growth of
    `zeta'` justify shifting to `Re s=-3`; the horizontal integrals vanish
    and the new vertical integral is absolutely `O(X^-3)`.  The crossed
    residues are `1/2` at `s=1`, none at zero, and
    `-3zeta(3)/(2pi)` at `s=-2`.  Consequently
    `S_w(X)=X/2-[3zeta(3)/(2pi)]X^-2+O(X^-3)`.
    This cancellation is algebraic and assumes no zero-free region.

    Exactly
    `S_w(X)=2sqrt(X)sum_q c_qK(x-logq)`.  Since
    `C(x)=(X+1)/(2sqrt X)`, the incoming prime degree is therefore
    `S_w(X)/(X+1)=X/[2(X+1)]+O(X^-3)`.  The outgoing degree is
    `O(X^4e^(-4pi X^2))` by the theta tail and `Lambda(n)<=log n`; hence
    `1/2-d_P(x)=1/[2(X+1)]+O(X^-3)`.

    Choose `eta=X^-10` and let `I_x` be a U-coordinate interval of width
    comparable to eta around x.  Define the arch rate away from this local
    contact by
    `a_A(x)=C(x)^(-1)int_(R\I_x)J(|x-y|)K(y)dy`.  For `y<=x/2`, exactly
    `C(x)^(-1)J(x-y)`
    `=2e^(y/2)/[(X+1)(1-X^-2e^(2y))]`.  Using
    `int e^(y/2)K(y)dy=int CK=1/4` and
    `int e^(5y/2)K(y)dy<inf` gives
    `a_A(x)=1/[2(X+1)]+O(X^-3)`; the omitted `y>x/2` and local-cut terms are
    superexponential even after their logarithmic J factor.  The one-theta
    tail gives `U'(x)=4pi X^2+O(1)`, and therefore
    `U'(x)[1/2-d_P(x)-a_A(x)]->0`.

    The remaining prime derivative moment is strictly positive.  With
    `w_1(r)=2r^(-1/2)K(logr)U'(logr)`, weighted PNT and Chebyshev domination
    give
    `A_P(x)=C(x)^(-1)sum_q c_q[K(x+logq)U'(x+logq)`
    `+K(x-logq)U'(x-logq)] -> A`, where
    `A=int_0^inf w_1(r)dr=2int_R e^(y/2)K(y)U'(y)dy>0`.
    Only ordinary PNT is used; positivity follows from the already-proved
    `U'>0`.  The analogous integrable weight with `|U|+1` proves the uniform
    bound `sum_q(|(r_q^+)'|+|(r_q^-)'|)=O(1)`.

    Construct the contact without a limiting or sampled LP.  Let `I_x` be a
    fixed-shape U-coordinate interval of width eta centered at `U(x)`.  Take
    a smooth compact U-bump supported in a slightly larger interval, of
    height below eta, U-slope at most one, and U-slope exactly `+1` on a
    neighborhood inside `I_x`.  Since the oriented
    target set `{U(x+/-logq)}` is discrete and `A_P(x)` is a convergent
    positive series, choose finitely many targets carrying all but
    `epsilon_x->0` of `A_P(x)`.  After fixing this finite set, add mutually
    disjoint, arbitrarily narrow
    smooth U-bumps whose slope is `+1` at exactly those targets and whose
    supports avoid every other target.  Their heights and arch derivative
    integrals can be made smaller than epsilon_x.  The resulting
    `f_x in C_c^infinity` satisfies `0<=f_x<eta` and
    `|df_x/dU|<=1`.

    Keep the local singularity compensated using the fixed-distance formula
    `L_Af(z)=C(z)^(-1)int_0^inf J(t){K(z+t)[f(z+t)-f(z)]`
    `+K(z-t)[f(z-t)-f(z)]}dt`.  Differentiate these bracketed differences
    before splitting any term; the divergent local arch rate is never
    separated.  On the smooth core this gives
    `(L_Af_x)'(x)=-U'(x)a_A(x)+o(1)`: inside `I_x` the equal contact slopes
    cancel the J singularity, the transition cost is
    `O(K(x)U'(x)/C(x))`, value terms are o(1), and the finitely many remote
    derivative integrals were chosen below epsilon_x.  Absolute
    differentiation of the prime series gives
    `(L_Pf_x)'(x)=-U'(x)d_P(x)+A_P(x)+o(1)`; its value terms are
    `O(eta)` by the preceding rate-derivative bound.  Hence exactly
    `(Lf_x)'(x)+U'(x)/2=A+o(1)>0`.

    Given b, choose x large enough that `eta<b` and this defect is positive.
    The oscillation of `f_x` is below b, so it respects every shortcut,
    while its U-slope bound respects the line cost.  For a sufficiently
    close pair in the central `+1` plateau with U-distance below b, every
    path using a shortcut costs at least b and every pure line path costs at
    least that U-distance.  Thus its graph distance is exactly the U-distance
    and `f_x` saturates it.  The positive derivative then gives
    `Lf_x(y)-Lf_x(x)+(1/2)d(x,y)>0` for a close endpoint y, contradicting
    rate-`1/2` W1 contraction.  This proves the no-go uniformly over every
    wild positive-floor cost system and uses neither floor attainment nor a
    finite-to-infinite numerical inference.

123. **Two independent chirp phases have a real cross-covariance escape from
    fixed-center tests, but phase alignment kills every fixed-ray version.**
    Let `T_j=Theta_j(x)`, `F_j=T_jB_j`, `q=F'_x`, and
    `G_j=T_j^(-alpha)F_j`.  The separated fixed-center tail vector is
    `Z_j=Theta'_jT_j^(-alpha)W_j=(1/(1-alpha))G'_j`, so the leading Gram is
    `Z(x)^TCq(y)`.  Fixed-center positivity places `C^(1/2)Z` in the dual
    cone generated by the earlier `C^(1/2)q` vectors.

    When C is diagonal this already closes the construction: the primitive
    energy
    `E=(1/2)sum_j c_jT_j^alpha G_j^2`
    satisfies
    `E'>=(alpha/2)sum_j c_j(Theta'_j/T_j)T_j^alpha G_j^2`, whereas exact
    normalization requires `E->0`.  For a non-diagonal two-channel C, the
    energy matrix is the Jordan product `(DC+CD)/2`,
    `D=diag(T_j^alpha)`.  Its positivity is equivalent to
    `|corr(C)|<=2sqrt(d_1d_2)/(d_1+d_2)`
    `=sech((1/2)log(d_1/d_2))`; incommensurate phases drive the right side to
    zero, exposing a possible fixed cross covariance.

    That algebraic possibility is real before all-pairs testing.  With
    `alpha=1/8`, `T_1=e^(4x)`, `T_2=e^(2x)`,
    `F=e^(.1x)(1,1)`, and
    `C=[[4,-3/2],[-3/2,1]]`, both primitive energies decay, q stays on one
    positive ray, and the dominant fixed-center component of Z is a negative
    second-channel vector; since `(C(1,1))_2=-1/2`, every fixed-center leading
    Gram has the required positive sign.  Thus independent phases do not
    formally reduce to item 121.

    The escape fails on an off-diagonal resonance.  On the curve
    `T_2(y)=T_1(x)` (here `y=2x`), the cross term is evaluated at phase
    difference zero and has leading coefficient
    `C_12q_1(x)q_2(y)<0`.  Both diagonal phase differences tend to infinity,
    so their covariance tails decay and the negative aligned cross term
    dominates, at physical separation `y-x->infinity` where the target
    majorant is positive.  More generally, for a fixed-ray profile `F=fv`,
    evading the diagonal-energy argument through channel k requires
    `v_k(Cv)_k<0`.  Since `C_kkv_k^2>0`, this forces
    `C_12v_1v_2<0`, exactly the sign exposed by a phase-alignment curve.
    Hence every fixed-ray two-independent-phase cross-covariance escape is
    closed.  Only rotating channel vectors or frequency-dependent cross
    spectra whose aligned coefficient is nonnegative at every phase remain.

124. **Phase-alignment signs close rotating two-phase amplitudes and
    frequency-dependent cross spectra.**  Write
    `q_j(x)=(Theta_jB_j)'` and
    `Q_j(T)=q_j(Theta_j^(-1)(T))`, and let `h_12(u)` be the full cross-channel
    covariance.  Along every far alignment curve
    `Theta_1(x)-Theta_2(y)=u`, the effective aligned coefficient is
    `Q_1(T)Q_2(T-u)h_12(u)`.  Item 123 shows that a negative value is exposed
    after the diagonal phase differences escape, so the all-pairs majorant
    requires this coefficient to be nonnegative for every fixed u.

    If `h_12` is nonzero near zero, make one fixed channel gauge with
    `h_12(0)>0`.  Two nearby offsets where it remains positive force the
    signs of `Q_2(T-u_1)` and `Q_2(T-u_2)` to agree for every sufficiently
    large T.  Continuity then makes both active `q_j` eventually
    constant-sign; after the gauge take `q_j>=0`.  The alignment condition at
    arbitrary u now forces `h_12(u)>=0` everywhere.  Thus a negative
    frequency band cannot hide: its negative covariance value is detected by
    the corresponding offset curve.  In particular, the regularly varying
    coefficient matrix C is entrywise nonnegative.

    Put `F_j=Theta_jB_j=int q_j>=0`,
    `G_j=Theta_j^(-alpha)F_j>=0`, and
    `Z=G'/(1-alpha)`.  Fixed-center matrix Karamata gives
    `Z(x)^TCq(y)>=0`.  Choose finitely many earlier y-values whose positive
    conic combination v has a positive coordinate on every active channel.
    Then `Cv` is strictly positive componentwise and
    `d/dx[G(x)^TCv]=(1-alpha)Z(x)^TCv>=0` eventually.  Exact primitive
    normalization gives `G(x)->0`, while `G>=0` makes the displayed
    projection strictly positive whenever G is nonzero.  A positive
    eventually nondecreasing function cannot tend to zero.  Hence G and the
    field are trivial.  This closes rotating amplitudes and arbitrary
    frequency-dependent two-channel cross spectra satisfying the necessary
    alignment signs.

125. **A cumulative-past projection closes countably many primitive
    channels under uniform operator asymptotics.**  After the alignment
    gauge, take `q(x),G(x)` coordinatewise nonnegative in `ell2`, with C an
    entrywise nonnegative bounded covariance operator and
    `G'=(1-alpha)Z`.  Choose a strictly positive integrable scalar weight w
    and define
    `v_x=int_(-inf)^(x-D)w(y)q(y)dy`.  Uniform separated-tail positivity
    gives `Z(x)^TCq(y)>=0` for every `y<=x-D`; integration yields
    `Z^TCv_x>=0`.  Therefore, almost everywhere,
    `Y'(x)=(1-alpha)Z^TCv_x`
    `+w(x-D)G^TCq(x-D)>=0`, where `Y=G^TCv_x`.

    Choose w so `v_x->v_inf` and `Cv_inf in ell2`.  Exact normalization
    `G->0` in `ell2` gives `Y->0`.  Since Y is nonnegative and nondecreasing,
    it vanishes identically.  If `Cv_inf` is positive on every channel ever
    active in the tail, this forces `G=0`.  An active coordinate outside its
    support would be an actual dark channel whose leading Gram against every
    past point is zero, not merely a weakly coupled channel; it cannot meet
    the strictly positive far majorant.  Operator-norm `o(1)` errors are
    handled first with compactly supported w by the same absorption as item
    121 and then by monotone exhaustion.  Failure of the Radon/`ell2`
    pairing or nonuniform-in-channel Karamata asymptotics would make the
    proposed Gram completion itself undefined on the claimed domain.
    Consequently neither finite nor countably infinite primitive chirp
    channels, including energy escaping to channel index, survive these
    natural domain hypotheses.

126. **Every regular rank-one non-primitive global correction fails by a
    zero-mean fixed-tail coefficient.**  Let `g_x=r(x)e_(Theta(x))` in a
    spectral Hilbert space with
    `<e_T,e_S>=h(T-S)`, where `r>0` is integrable, Theta is unbounded, and
    `h(u)->0`.  Put `m=int g_xdx` and `M=int r`.

    The envelope-preserving weighted centering is
    `Psi_x=r(x)(e_(Theta(x))-bar e)`, `bar e=m/M`.  It satisfies
    `int Psi=0`, and its PSD Gram, divided by `r_xr_y`, is
    `h(T_x-T_y)-H(T_x)-H(T_y)+H_0`, where
    `H(T)=<e_T,bar e>` and `H_0=||bar e||^2`.  For fixed y and
    `x->infinity`, weak covariance decay gives the limit `H_0-H(T_y)`.  But
    exactly
    `int[H_0-H(T_y)]r_y dy=MH_0-<m,bar e>=0`.  Unless this coefficient
    vanishes identically, it is negative at some fixed y and the far
    covariance is eventually negative where the required majorant is
    positive.  If it vanishes, unbounded phase support and `H(T)->0` force
    `H_0=0` and `m=0`.  For a spectral density positive on an interval this
    is impossible: the Fourier amplitude defining m is nonzero near
    frequency zero because its value at zero is M.

    The pointwise orthogonal projection has the same obstruction.  Set
    `Psi_x=P_(m^perp)g_x`
    `=g_x-<g_x,m>m/||m||^2`; again `int Psi=0`.  If
    `h(T)~cT^(-alpha)`, `0<alpha<1`, then
    `<e_T,m>~McT^(-alpha)`, and the fixed-y leading coefficient of the Gram
    is `1-H(T_y)/H_0` after harmless normalization.  Its r-weighted mean is
    zero, so it too has a forbidden negative value unless it vanishes
    identically, which reduces to the centering case.

    More generally take `Psi_x=g_x-w(x)m` with `int w=1`.  If `w_x/r_x`
    has a nonzero tail subsequence, the dominant fixed-y coefficient is
    `A(y)=w_y||m||^2-r_y<e_(T_y),m>` and `int A=0`.  If `w_x/r_x->0`, regular
    variation instead gives the coefficient `r_y-Mw_y`, also of integral
    zero.  A nonzero coefficient is negative somewhere; the two possible
    identically-zero cases are precisely the orthogonal projection and
    weighted centering already excluded.  Thus scalar global centering,
    rank-one orthogonal projection, and every regular rank-one correction
    fail without any zeta-zero or RH input.

127. **Exact centering plus far positivity forbids every nonzero weak tail
    profile.**  Let H be a Hilbert space, let `r>0` be integrable, and let
    `psi_x` be strongly measurable with the Bochner normalization
    `int r(x)psi_xdx=0`.  Assume
    `<psi_x,psi_y>>=0` whenever `|x-y|>=D`, as every feature majorant must
    because `q(|x-y|)>0` there.  If for some `x_n->infinity` and positive
    scales `tau_n` one had
    `psi_(x_n)/tau_n -> v` weakly with `v!=0`, then for each fixed y the
    far-pair sign would give `<v,psi_y>>=0`.  Bochner integration yields
    `int r(y)<v,psi_y>dy=<v,int r psi>=0`; hence the nonnegative integrand
    vanishes almost everywhere.  Thus v is orthogonal to the closed span of
    `{psi_y}`.  But every `psi_(x_n)/tau_n` belongs to that span and a closed
    subspace is weakly closed, so v belongs to it as well.  Therefore v=0, a
    contradiction.

    In particular, normalize any unbounded sequence by
    `tau_n=||psi_(x_n)||`.  Weak compactness says that every weak cluster
    point must be zero.  This is impossible in finite dimension, or whenever
    the normalized tail set is norm precompact.  Hence no finite-dimensional
    feature completion and no compact direction field can combine exact
    centering with the required far positivity.  An escape needs normalized
    directions converging weakly to zero through genuinely infinite Hilbert
    dimension.  The Bochner hypothesis is material: a merely distributional
    or conditionally convergent normalization must be audited separately and
    is the remaining global-divergence possibility.

128. **Finite-rank and regular Hilbert--Schmidt global corrections fail at
    their first fixed-tail scale.**  Let `mu(dy)=r(y)dy` have finite positive
    mass M, put `e_x=e_(Theta(x))`, and assume a common orbit tail
    `<e_x,e_y>/s_x->1` for every fixed y, where `s_x->0`.  Let
    `B:R^N->H` be injective after discarding its kernel and assume
    `B^*e_x/s_x->beta`.  For a continuous coefficient `a(x)`, define
    `psi_x=e_x-Ba(x)` and impose the Bochner normalization
    `int psi_xdmu=0`.  Write `b=int a dmu`; then `Bb=int e_xdmu`.
    The dominated tail condition
    `int r(y)|<e_x,e_y>/s_x-1|dy->0`
    implies the exact compatibility `<beta,b>=M`.

    Put `delta_x=max{s_x,||Ba(x)||}`.  Along any tail sequence,
    finite-dimensional compactness supplies a subsequence such that
    `s_x/delta_x->c in [0,1]` and
    `Ba(x)/delta_x->v`, with `max{c,||v||}=1`.  Dividing the Gram row by
    delta gives, for fixed y, the coefficient
    `A(y)=c[1-<beta,a(y)>]-<v,e_y-Ba(y)>`.  Its weighted mean is exactly
    zero:
    `int A dmu=c[M-<beta,b>]-<v,int psi dmu>=0`.
    Far-pair positivity makes `A(y)>=0`; continuity and `r>0` therefore force
    `A` to vanish identically.

    Both scale regimes contradict that conclusion.  If `c>0`, then
    `delta_x->0`; injectivity of B gives `a(x)->0`, while
    `B^*e_x=O(s_x)` gives `<v,e_x>->0`.  Evaluating the identically-zero
    formula at the moving x yields `A(x)->c>0`.  If `c=0`, then `||v||=1`
    and `A=0` says `<v,psi_y>=0` for every y.  Write `v=Balpha`; after division
    by delta,
    `<v,e_x>/delta=(s_x/delta)<alpha,B^*e_x/s_x>->0`, whereas
    `<v,Ba(x)>/delta->||v||^2=1`, a contradiction.  Hence every first
    fixed-tail coefficient is zero-mean but nonzero and must be negative
    somewhere.  This includes weighted centering, finite-rank orthogonal
    projection, and arbitrary continuous finite-rank global corrections.

    The same proof extends to a Hilbert--Schmidt map `B:E->H`.  Assume
    `B^*e_x/s_x->beta` strongly in E, `a in L1(mu;E)`, and the domain/no-index-
    escape bound
    `sup_(x>=x_0)||a(x)||_E/[s_x+||Ba(x)||_H]<inf`.
    Then `a(x)/delta_x` is bounded; after weak subsequence convergence,
    compactness of B makes `Ba(x)/delta_x` converge strongly.  The preceding
    coefficient and zero-mean identity are unchanged.  In the `c>0` case the
    domain bound forces `a(x)->0`; in the `c=0` case the strong B-image limit
    gives the same `0-1` contradiction.  Thus Hilbert--Schmidt corrections
    fail as well.  The only escape is a non-Bochner/global-divergence
    normalization, failure of the common fixed-tail limits, or singular
    coefficient energy escaping so that the displayed domain bound fails.

129. **A genuine separating zero-floor shortcut metric exists, but an exact
    nonlocal torus contact excludes it.**  Put `ell=log 2`,
    `alpha=pi sqrt(2)/ell`, `beta=pi sqrt(3)/ell`, and
    `Phi(x)=(cos(alpha x),sin(alpha x),cos(beta x),sin(beta x))`.  Its speed is
    `pi sqrt(5)/ell<11<18<U'(x)`.  For the dyadic edge
    `z<->z+n ell` assign the translation-invariant cost
    `A_n=||Phi(z+n ell)-Phi(z)||`
    `=2[sin^2(n pi sqrt(2)/2)+sin^2(n pi sqrt(3)/2)]^(1/2)`; assign every
    other prime-power edge its `d_U` cost and take the induced graph metric d.
    Every generating edge costs at least its Phi chord, while every dyadic
    edge costs exactly that chord.  Hence
    `d(x,y)>=||Phi(x)-Phi(y)||`.  The map Phi is injective, because equality at
    two points with nonzero displacement would make `sqrt(2/3)` rational, so
    d separates points.  Moreover `d(z,z+n ell)=A_n` exactly.  No `A_n`
    vanishes, while Kronecker's theorem and the Q-independence of
    `1,sqrt(2)/2,sqrt(3)/2` give `inf_n A_n=0`.  Thus this is an everywhere
    zero-floor, noncollapsed system and a counterexample to the proposed
    separation-implies-positive-floor dichotomy.

    The metric still has the hypotheses needed by item 94.  It is continuous
    because `d<=d_U`; it has finite mu-second moment; and bounded d-Lipschitz
    functions are dense in `L2(mu)`.  For the last claim, torus trigonometric
    polynomials pull back to bounded d-Lipschitz functions.  If `g in L2(mu)`
    is orthogonal to all of them, the Fourier transform of the finite measure
    `g mu` vanishes on `{m alpha+n beta:m,n in Z}`, a dense subgroup because
    `alpha/beta` is irrational.  Continuity and uniqueness of the Fourier
    transform then give `g=0`.  In sharp contrast with item 122, the only
    compactly supported d-Lipschitz function is zero: along a Kronecker
    subsequence `A_(n_k)->0`, Lipschitzness and eventual vanishing at
    `z+n_k ell` force the value at every fixed z to vanish.

    Nevertheless a single nonlocal contact excludes this metric.  Let
    `h=ell/2`, `A=A_1`, `s_j=sin(pi sqrt(j)/2)` for `j=2,3`, and
    `f(x)=[2s_2 sin(alpha x)+2s_3 sin(beta x)]/A`.  This is a unit linear
    functional of Phi, hence `Lip_d(f)<=1`; algebraically
    `f(+/-h)=+/-A/2`, and the dyadic edge together with the Phi lower bound
    gives `d(-h,h)=A`.  Reflection invariance and oddness reduce the necessary
    infinitesimal rate-`1/2` contact inequality to
    `2Lf(h)+A/2<=0`.

    The deterministic 180-bit Arb certificate
    `scripts/zero_floor_torus_shortcut_certificate.py` instead proves
    `2Lf(h)+A/2>4/25`.  It evaluates the q=2 and q=3 terms directly, encloses
    every q>=4 term using `Lambda(q)/sqrt(q)<=1`, theta logarithmic decay
    above 10, and `sum_(n>=4)(4/n)^10<13/9`, and encloses the archimedean
    integral by interval cells.  The omitted singular neighborhood uses
    `J(r)<1/r` and `|f'|<=|Phi'|`; the two spatial tails use theta decay above
    100.  The independently rerun output is the strict interval
    `[0.1686810499...,0.1758058386...]`.  The arch integral is absolutely
    convergent at the contact because `f(y)-f(h)=O(|y-h|)`; finite Levy
    cutoffs, differentiation at zero, and dominated convergence justify the
    generator contact with no domain assumption.  Therefore sharp W1
    contraction fails for this explicit genuine zero-floor system.  The
    certificate is a finite falsifier for this model, not a finite-to-infinite
    inference about arbitrary costs.

130. **Even a zero-floor infinite Hilbert torus with no finite-character
    shortcut contact fails at remote exposed faces.**  Let `p_j` be the j-th
    prime, `w_j=2^(-j)`,
    `omega_j=1/100+10^(-(j+30))sqrt(p_j)`,
    `t_j=2pi omega_j/log2`, and
    `Phi_inf(x)=(w_j cos(t_jx),w_j sin(t_jx))_(j>=1)` in `ell2`.  The
    multiquadratic sign automorphisms prove that
    `{1,omega_1,...,omega_J}` is Q-independent for every J.  Bertrand's
    bound gives `0<omega_j-1/100<2*10^(-31)`, and the curve speed is below
    one, hence below `U'`.

    Assign `z<->z+n log2` the Hilbert chord
    `a_n=2[sum_j w_j^2 sin^2(pi n omega_j)]^(1/2)` and every nondyadic
    prime-power edge its `d_U` cost.  Finite-prefix Kronecker approximation
    followed by the `ell2` tail bound gives `inf_n a_n=0`; every `a_n>0`, the
    chord sequence is subadditive, and `Phi_inf` is injective.  As in item
    129, the induced metric d therefore satisfies
    `d>=||Phi_inf(x)-Phi_inf(y)||`, separates points, and has
    `d(z,z+n log2)=a_n` exactly.

    This model defeats every finite-character shortcut test.  The dyadic
    orbit completes to the full weighted Hilbert torus `T^N`, since every
    finite projection is dense.  A continuous character of `T^N` uses only
    finitely many coordinates: continuity makes it map a tail subgroup into
    a proper identity arc, and the only subgroup of the circle in that arc is
    trivial.  If a 1-Lipschitz contact f depends on a finite coordinate set S,
    extend it to the completion and compare two points having the actual
    S-coordinate displacement but identical tail coordinates.  Then
    `|f(g+n)-f(g)|<a_n`, strictly because every omitted coordinate moves.
    Thus no finite trigonometric contact saturates any dyadic shortcut.

    The canonical infinite contact does saturate.  Put `r_n=n log2/2`,
    `Delta_n=Phi_inf(r_n)-Phi_inf(-r_n)`, and
    `F_n(x)=<Delta_n/a_n,Phi_inf(x)>`.  It is bounded, smooth, 1-Lipschitz,
    and has `F_n(r_n)-F_n(-r_n)=a_n`.  For
    `kappa_n(t)=L(sin(t .))(r_n)/sin(t r_n)+1/2` and
    `A_(j,n)=2w_j|sin(pi n omega_j)|`, direct linearity gives the exact
    defect
    `D_n=LF_n(r_n)-LF_n(-r_n)+a_n/2`
    `=(1/a_n)sum_j A_(j,n)^2 kappa_n(t_j)`.
    The updated 180-bit certificate
    `scripts/zero_floor_torus_shortcut_certificate.py` proves uniformly over
    the entire phase band that `kappa_1<-1/5000`; hence even the q=2
    canonical infinite contact has the required nonpositive sign.

    Remote edges reverse it.  Let
    `beta^+(dy)=2exp(-y/2)K(y)dy` and
    `M(t)=int sin(ty) beta^+(dy)`
    `=-4int_0^inf K(y)sinh(y/2)sin(ty)dy`.  The same Arb certificate proves
    `M(t)<-1/1000` uniformly on the phase band, with the spatial tail enclosed
    by theta decay.  Ordinary weighted PNT, uniformly on this compact t-band
    by a common theta-Schwartz majorant and a finite-net/Lipschitz argument,
    gives
    `L sin(t .)(x)=M(t)-(1/2)sin(tx)+o(1)` as `x->infinity`.  The exact
    endpoint term cancels the second summand, so
    `D_n=(4/a_n)sum_j w_j^2 sin(pi n omega_j)M(t_j)+o(1)`.

    Kronecker supplies an unbounded sequence `n_k` on which
    `sin(pi n_k omega_j)<=-1/2` for `j<=6`.  Write
    `H=sum_(j<=6)4^(-j)=(1-4^(-6))/3` and
    `T=sum_(j>=7)4^(-j)=4^(-6)/3`.  The head of the last sum is at least
    `H/2000`, while `|M|<=beta^+(R)=1/2` bounds the tail loss by `T/2`.
    Their difference is
    `[1-1001/4096]/6000>0`, and the same six coordinates bound `a_(n_k)`
    away from zero.  Hence `D_(n_k)>0` for every sufficiently large k,
    contradicting sharp W1 contraction.  No sampled asymptotic or finite
    character extrapolation is used.

    More generally, if `sigma=beta^+-beta^-` and `E_n` is the exposed face of
    unit Lipschitz functions saturating a remote shortcut, tail convergence
    makes the live gate
    `limsup_n sup_(f in E_n) int f dsigma<=0` for every uniformly dominated
    contact family.  Proving this polar inclusion for an arbitrary zero-floor
    norm is the exact remaining state-independent shortcut lemma.

131. **The smallest genuine two-point divergence has the exact sign geometry,
    but fixed-lag theta amplification kills it; literal Bochner centering
    then creates an explicit negative far row.**  Let
    `D=log p`, where `p^3-p-1=0`, put
    `u_+=(5+sqrt(13))/2`, and first take `a=D/u_+`.  Set
    `p_a(t)=exp(-|t|/a)/(2a)`, so
    `hat p_a(xi)=(1+a^2 xi^2)^(-1)`.  In
    `H=L2(R,dxi/(2pi))` define
    `e_x(xi)=exp(i xi x)/(1+a^2 xi^2)^(1/2)`; then
    `<e_x,e_y>=p_a(x-y)`.  The genuinely two-point potential
    `A(x,y)=p_a(x-y)(e_x-e_y)` is antisymmetric, and its graph divergence is
    `Psi_x=int A(x,y)dy=e_x-int p_a(x-y)e_y dy`.  Hence exactly
    `Psi_x(xi)=exp(i xi x)a^2xi^2/(1+a^2xi^2)^(3/2)`.

    Its Gram is positive semidefinite by construction and has both the
    Fourier and direct-convolution formulas
    `G_a(t)=int exp(i xi t)a^4xi^4/(1+a^2xi^2)^3 dxi/(2pi)`
    `=p_a(t)-2(p_a*p_a)(t)+(p_a*p_a*p_a)(t)`.
    Writing `u=|t|/a`, the elementary identities
    `(p_a*p_a)(t)=exp(-u)(u+1)/(4a)` and
    `(p_a*p_a*p_a)(t)=exp(-u)(u^2+3u+3)/(16a)` give
    `G_a(t)=exp(-u)(u^2-5u+3)/(16a)`.
    Thus `G_a` is negative exactly for
    `a(5-sqrt(13))/2<|t|<a(5+sqrt(13))/2=D` and positive for every
    `|t|>D`; taking `a` slightly smaller gives strict positivity also at D.
    This is the smallest explicit PSD kernel found with negative correlations
    only inside the plastic window and positive correlations at every farther
    pair.  For `Phi_x=Psi_x/K(x)`,
    `||Phi_x||^2=3/[16aK(x)^2]`, so it has the required reciprocal-theta
    feature scale and is infinite-rank rather than a primitive scalar
    derivative or finite-rank correction.

    The far magnitude is not the obstruction.  If
    `M_K(t)=sup_x K(x)K(x+t)`, then
    `sup_(t>D) q(t)M_K(t)/G_a(t)<infinity`.  Near D this follows from the
    simple positive zeros of q and `G_a`.  At infinity use
    `M_K(t)<=K(0)K(t/2)`,
    `K(t/2)<=4pi^2 exp(9t/4-pi exp(t))`, and
    `G_a(t) asymp t^2 exp(-t/a)`; the quotient tends to zero.  Therefore one
    constant rescaling makes `G_a/[K(x)K(y)]>=q(|x-y|)` at every far pair.
    But at the exact fixed lag `t=a`,
    `G_a(a)=-exp(-1)/(16a)`, and consequently
    `G_a(a)/[K(x)K(x+a)]->-infinity`, whereas the finite number `q(a)` is
    negative because `a<D`.  No nonzero rescaling can satisfy the required
    lower bound on this same-tail row.

    This fixed-lag argument is a general stationary-divergence lemma, not a
    peculiarity of the Laplace choice.  If
    `Psi_x(xi)=exp(i xi x)m(xi)` is a nonzero stationary Hilbert field whose
    continuous covariance G is integrable and whose exact zero-frequency
    condition is `m(0)=0`, then `int_R G(t)dt=||m(0)||^2=0` while
    `G(0)>0`.  Hence G is negative at some nonzero fixed lag `t_0`.  If
    `|t_0|>=D`, far positivity already fails; if `|t_0|<D`, division by
    `K(x)K(x+t_0)` sends that row to minus infinity.  The lemma includes
    every finite positive mixture of scales and every constant finite
    cross-channel mixing: after factorization it is still one vector
    multiplier m.  Thus finite stationary mixtures cannot repair the local
    row.

    Antisymmetry by itself gives distributional, not Bochner, cancellation.
    For the sharp cutoff `S_R=int_(-R)^R Psi_xdx`, direct integration gives
    `||S_R||^2=2int_0^(2R)(2R-t)G_a(t)dt`
    `=(a/8)[1+exp(-L)(L^2-L-1)]`, `L=2R/a`, and hence the limit is `a/8`.
    More generally a stationary multiplier with `m/xi in L2` has
    `lim ||S_R||^2=2int ||m(xi)||^2/xi^2 dxi/(2pi)>0` unless it is zero.
    This observation prevents cancellation by a finite stationary mixture,
    but it is not a closed-form domain obstruction.

    Indeed put `M(x)=C(x)K(x)` and, in the ground coordinate `f=Ku`, define
    on `C_c^infinity subset L2(M dx)`
    `r[u]=int g_a(xi)|hat u(xi)|^2dxi/(2pi)`,
    `g_a=a^4xi^4/(1+a^2xi^2)^3=|m_a|^2`.  This form is closable.  For
    `gamma in C_c^infinity` with
    `int gamma=int x gamma=0`, let
    `v=m_a(D)^(-1)gamma`.  Since `m_a(xi)~a^2xi^2` at zero and
    `m_a(xi)~1/(a|xi|)` at infinity, `v in L2(dx)` and
    `m_a(D)v=gamma`; moreover
    `<m_a(D)u,v>_(L2(dx))=<u,gamma/M>_(L2(Mdx))`, so v lies in the adjoint
    domain.  Such v are dense: a vector orthogonal to all of them would,
    after division by `m_a(D)`, be an affine distribution, which `m_a(D)`
    annihilates because of its double zero, and hence would be zero.  Thus
    the analysis operator and r are closable.

    If `chi in C_c^infinity` equals one near zero and
    `chi_R(x)=chi(x/R)`, then `chi_R->1` in `L2(Mdx)` and
    `r[chi_R]=R int g_a(eta/R)|hat chi(eta)|^2deta/(2pi)`
    `=a^4R^(-3)||chi''||_2^2+o(R^(-3))`.  Hence `u=1`, equivalently f=K,
    belongs to the closed zero-energy domain.  The sharp-cutoff boundary
    norm therefore records escaping boundary energy, but does not invalidate
    the exact extended-form normalization.  The fixed negative lag above is
    the fatal obstruction.

    A literal Bochner-centered repair also fails exactly.  Put
    `w(x)=exp(-|x|/a)` and
    `A_w(x,y)=w(x)w(y)p_a(x-y)(e_x-e_y)`.  This potential is absolutely
    Bochner integrable and antisymmetric, so
    `Psi_x^w=int A_w(x,y)dy` satisfies `int Psi_x^w dx=0` by Fubini.  Write
    `c_x=(p_a*w)(x)` and
    `v_x=int p_a(x-y)w(y)e_y dy`; then
    `Psi_x^w=w(x)(c_xe_x-v_x)`.  For `x>=0`, `u=x/a`, one has
    `c_x=exp(-u)(u+1)/2` and, exactly,
    `<e_x,Psi_0^w>=[p_(a/2)(x)-p_a(x)]/6`.
    The convolution identity
    `<v_x,Psi_0^w>`
    `=p_a*(w<e_.,Psi_0^w>)(x)`
    `=p_a(x)/72+p_(a/2)(x)/36-p_(a/3)(x)/72`
    then yields the exact center-tail row
    `<Psi_x^w,Psi_0^w>`
    `=a^(-1)[-exp(-2u)/144-(3u+5)exp(-3u)/72`
    `                     +(4u+5)exp(-4u)/48]`.
    It is negative for all sufficiently large x and is asymptotic to
    `-exp(-2x/a)/(144a)`.  After division by `K(x)K(0)` it tends to minus
    infinity where `q(x)>0`.  Meanwhile
    `||Psi_x^w|| asymp x exp(-2x/a)`, so
    `||Psi_x^w/K(x)||` still has reciprocal-theta dominant scale.  Thus the
    simplest genuinely two-point, literally centered envelope loses the far
    sign rather than the tail size.

    A scalar `L1` envelope cannot center the unweighted stationary field:
    `int s(x)Psi_xdx=m_a(xi)hat s(-xi)`, and `m_a(xi)!=0` for `xi!=0`
    forces `s=0`.  Nor does an adiabatic variable Laplace scale repair the
    reciprocal-theta candidate.  If a frozen local scale `a_x` and amplitude
    `sigma_x` give the natural moving-lag asymptotic
    `<Psi_x,Psi_(x+a_x)>=-exp(-1)sigma_xsigma_(x+a_x)/(16a_x)(1+o(1))`,
    then `q(a_x)~-1/(2a_x)` forces
    `sigma_xsigma_(x+a_x)<=8eK(x)K(x+a_x)(1+o(1))`.
    Scale collapse can remove the sharp boundary flux only by making the
    local amplitude theta-small, contrary to the unscaled reciprocal-theta
    field.  This closes the stationary finite-mixture, scalar-envelope, and
    regular adiabatic-scale versions of the two-point divergence.  A survivor
    would need genuinely nonstationary infinite-channel/index escape, with
    normalized directions weakly tending to zero, no fixed negative lag, and
    local negative amplitude already calibrated at the `K(x)K(y)` scale;
    that singular global-divergence class is not excluded here.

132. **A one-scale singular index transition has too little local negative
    capacity; every locally closable multiscale repair is annihilated by the
    full threshold module.**  Let `D=log p`, let
    `q(t)=2cosh(t/2)-J(t)`, and suppose a strongly measurable Hilbert field
    `Phi_x` has the literal Bochner normalization
    `int K(x)Phi_x dx=0`.  Put `R(x,y)=<Phi_x,Phi_y>` and assume the required
    pointwise majorant `R(x,y)>=q(|x-y|)` off the diagonal.

    Fix a central interval `I=[-a,a]`.  For all sufficiently large x,
    `x-y>=log 2` on I, so the sharp item-81 bound gives
    `q(x-y)>=(5/6)exp((x-y)/2)`.  Consequently
    `P_x=int_I K(y)R(x,y)dy>=c_a exp(x/2)`, where
    `c_a=(5/6)int_I K(y)exp(-y/2)dy>0`.  Every term with
    `|x-y|>=D` is nonnegative.  Taking the inner product of `Phi_x` with the
    Bochner normalization therefore forces the exact local-capacity bound
    `int_(|h|<D) K(x+h)[-R(x,x+h)]_+ dh>=c_a exp(x/2)`.

    On the other hand, the majorant itself and
    `J(h)=exp(-h/2)/(1-exp(-2h))<1/h` imply pointwise
    `[-R(x,x+h)]_+<1/|h|`.  Define the logarithmic negative support
    `L_x=int_({0<|h|<D:R(x,x+h)<0}) dh/|h|`.  Since K is decreasing on the
    positive tail,
    `L_x>=c_a exp(x/2)/K(x-D)`.  In particular, if the row is nonnegative
    for `0<|h|<epsilon_x`, then necessarily
    `epsilon_x<=D exp[-c_a exp(x/2)/(2K(x-D))]`.  This is not an ordinary
    shrinking transition scale: its logarithm already has
    reciprocal-theta size.

    The bound immediately excludes every one-scale transition.  More
    precisely, suppose for one fixed `C>1` the negative part of the x-row is
    supported in
    `epsilon_x<=|h|<=C epsilon_x`, and assume the adiabatic domain condition
    `omega_x=sup_(|h|<=Cepsilon_x)|log K(x+h)-log K(x)|->0`.  Then its total
    negative capacity is at most
    `2exp(omega_x)K(x)log C`, contradicting the preceding
    `c_a exp(x/2)` lower bound.  This conclusion is independent of the
    feature norm and of how quickly its channel index changes.  In the
    equivalent norm/width audit, a fixed-fraction negative contact
    `R(x,x+epsilon)<=-kappa||Phi_x||||Phi_(x+epsilon)||` must obey
    `kappa||Phi_x||||Phi_(x+epsilon)||<=1/epsilon`; a lobe of width
    `O(epsilon)` can therefore contribute only `O(K(x))`, never the required
    `exp(x/2)`.  Thus the stationary, adiabatic, single-burst, and
    piecewise-constant index-escape candidates all fail.  A survivor needs
    negative support on a logarithmically enormous hierarchy of scales down
    to the displayed epsilon bound.

    A concrete infinite-channel search shows why far signs alone are
    misleading.  Put `lambda=1/2`,
    `F_j(theta)=|sum_(k=0)^(j-1)e^(iktheta)|^2`, and
    `g(theta)=6-sum_(j>=2)lambda^jF_j(theta)>=1/2`, with equality only at
    `theta=0`.  Its Fourier coefficients
    are `c_0=9/2` and `c_d=-2lambda^d` for `d>=1`.  In
    `L2(T,g dtheta/(2pi))`, take `z_n=e^(i n theta)`,
    `gamma_n=(100+|n|)^(-2)`, and
    `v_n=gamma_nz_n-gamma_(n+1)z_(n+1)`.  The series `sum_n v_n` converges
    absolutely to zero.  With `rho_n=gamma_(n+1)/gamma_n`, one has
    `|rho_n-1|<=0.0201` and, for `d=m-n>=2`,
    `<v_n,v_m>=2gamma_ngamma_mlambda^(d-1)`
    ` [rho_n+lambda^2rho_m-lambda(1+rho_nrho_m)]`
    `>=0.486gamma_ngamma_mlambda^(d-1)>0`.
    Thus the normalized directions genuinely escape weakly through channel
    index while every nonadjacent Gram entry has the desired sign.  However
    `<v_n,v_(n+1)><-5gamma_ngamma_(n+1)`.  If fixed-width blocks have theta
    masses `mu_n` and the field is `t v_n/mu_n` on block n, adjacent-row
    admissibility forces
    `t^2<=(-Q)mu_nmu_(n+1)/(5gamma_ngamma_(n+1))`, where
    `Q=sup_(0<s<2ell)q(s)<0`; the right side tends to zero in both theta
    tails.  Far domination requires a fixed positive lower bound on `t^2`,
    so the scaling interval is empty.  This is an exact falsifier of the
    claim that Bochner centering, weak index escape, and correct far signs
    suffice.

    Exact centering is only the first null constraint.  For every even
    polynomial p the threshold vector `f_p=p(-D^2)K` satisfies
    `Q_W[f_p]=0`.  If a proposed completion gave a valid positive split
    `Q_W=B+R`, with `R[f]=||A_Rf||^2` and B the cooperative positive part,
    then
    `0=B[f_p]+||A_Rf_p||^2` forces `A_Rf_p=0` for every p.  By item 52, the
    restrictions of this threshold module are dense in even `L2` on every
    compact interval.  Hence any analysis map whose compact restrictions
    are `L2`-closable and for which the threshold approximation localizes in
    its graph norm must vanish on every compact even core; exhaustion gives
    `A_R=0`, contradicting the strictly positive far majorant.  The proposed
    singular cascade must therefore be genuinely non-locally-closable, not
    merely a Hilbert--Schmidt synthesis with badly conditioned
    coefficients.

    This also identifies the domain circularity.  If such a multiscale
    kernel were nevertheless shown to make `R-q` a locally dominated Radon
    jump measure, to put K and all `f_p` in the zero-energy extended domain,
    and to leave no derivative-of-delta diagonal residue, then the item-81
    ground-state/Picone bridge would prove `Q_W>=0`.  Verifying those
    properties is therefore the original RH-strength positivity problem,
    not a formal consequence of PSD Gram factorization.  The sole remainder
    is a logarithmically multiscale, non-locally-closable global-divergence
    cascade; no one-scale or ordinary singular-value index escape survives.

133. **The theta-scale split sharpens the singular transition to a
    reciprocal-`K` logarithmic budget; a Sobolev no-go is valid only under an
    explicit extra domain hypothesis.**  Retain item 132's Bochner-centered
    Gram field and put
    `N_x={h:0<|h|<D, R(x,x+h)<0}` and `eta_x=exp(-2x)`.  The positive theta
    expansion gives, for one absolute `C_0` and all sufficiently large x,
    `sup_(|h|<=eta_x)K(x+h)<=C_0K(x)`.  Indeed the `n=1` summand has exponent
    `-pi exp(2x)`, whose change on this window is bounded, and the sum of the
    `n>=2` summands is uniformly negligible relative to it.  On the remaining
    local window, monotonicity of the positive tail and `[-R]_+<=-q<J<1/|h|`
    give
    `int_(eta_x<=|h|<D)K(x+h)[-R(x,x+h)]_+dh`
    ` <=2K(x-D)log(D/eta_x)=O(xK(x-D))=o(exp(x/2))`.

    Subtracting this medium-lag contribution from item 132's exact row
    capacity and using the small-window theta comparison proves, for some
    fixed `c>0`,
    `int_(N_x intersect {|h|<=eta_x}) dh/|h|`
    `                         >=c exp(x/2)/K(x)`                     `(133.1)`
    for every sufficiently large x.  Thus a survivor needs negative
    correlations on a set of logarithmic lag measure of reciprocal-theta
    size, not merely a negative correlation at one reciprocal-theta-small
    lag.  Any finite union of bands whose total logarithmic width is
    `o(exp(x/2)/K(x))` is excluded.  In particular, every lobe of width
    `O(epsilon_x)` around one scale `epsilon_x` has bounded logarithmic width
    and cannot perform the row cancellation, regardless of feature norm or
    channel-index speed.

    If `Phi` is strongly continuous, define the first negative lag
    `rho_x=inf{|h|:0<|h|<=eta_x, R(x,x+h)<0}`.  The diagonal is positive and
    (133.1) makes the negative set nonempty, so `0<rho_x<eta_x`; since the
    logarithmic measure on the two sides is at most
    `2log(eta_x/rho_x)`,
    `rho_x<=exp(-2x)exp[-c exp(x/2)/K(x)]`                 `(133.2)`
    after changing c.  From
    `K(x)=2pi^2 exp(9x/2-pi exp(2x))(1+o(1))`, the exponent in (133.2) is
    asymptotic, up to a positive constant, to
    `exp(pi exp(2x)-4x)`.  This is strictly stronger than the coarse
    `K(x-D)` first-lag bound in item 132.

    There is a further obstruction under the following explicitly named
    **theta-Sobolev hypothesis**, which is not a consequence of form
    closability: `Phi` is locally absolutely continuous and there is a
    positive weight w such that
    `int w(s)||Phi'_s||^2ds<infinity` and, with
    `w_*(x)=inf_(|s-x|<=eta_x)w(s)`, one has
    `-log w_*(x)=o(exp(x/2)/K(x))`.  Choose a negative lag with length at most
    `2rho_x`.  Cauchy--Schwarz and
    `<Phi_x,Phi_(x+h)><0` give
    `int_[x,x+h]||Phi'_s||^2ds`
    ` >=||Phi_(x+h)-Phi_x||^2/|h|>=||Phi_x||^2/(2rho_x)`.
    Moreover `||Phi_x||||Phi_0||>=R(x,0)>=q(x)`, so
    `||Phi_x||>=c_1exp(x/2)`.  Multiplication by `w_*(x)` and (133.2) makes
    the displayed weighted energy tend to infinity, contradicting the
    theta-Sobolev hypothesis.  Powers of K times ordinary exponential
    weights all satisfy the stated logarithmic condition.  Along a genuine
    weak-index-escape sequence, item 127 sharpens the norm statement to
    `||Phi_x||/exp(x/2)->infinity`, because
    `<Phi_x/||Phi_x||,Phi_y> >=c_y exp(x/2)/||Phi_x||` for every fixed y.

    The full threshold-null constraint remains essential and is independent
    of (133.1).  A valid positive form split `Q_W=B+R`, with
    `R[f]=||A_Rf||^2`, must satisfy
    `A_R[p(-D^2)K]=0` for every polynomial p, not only `A_RK=0`, because all
    these threshold vectors have exact zero `Q_W` energy and both summands
    are nonnegative.  Item 52's compact even-`L2` density then kills the
    analysis map only if those approximations converge in its graph norm.
    Mere closedness does not choose the relevant topology: the natural
    ground-coordinate space is `L2(C/K dx)`, not ordinary `L2(dx)`, and
    approximants may carry uncontrolled analysis norm in the remote tails.
    Likewise closability alone supplies no absolute continuity or
    theta-Sobolev bound for `Phi`.  Item 134 gives both the exact weighted
    counterexample and the smallest additional closure hypothesis under
    which global threshold density does become decisive.

    Finally, off-diagonal domination is not by itself the Picone bridge.  A
    candidate must still prove that `Q_W-R` is a closed lower-semibounded
    cooperative form, that `R-q` is a valid local Radon jump measure, that K
    and the threshold module lie in the required (possibly extended) common
    domains, and that no derivative-of-delta diagonal residue remains.  A
    merely naturally closed global logarithmic cascade can evade the
    conditional Sobolev and ordinary-`L2` localization conclusions, but
    verifying these stronger common-domain facts would already furnish the
    complete positive split.
    Thus the only covariance remainder is an all-log-scales, ultraviolet-
    order, globally nonlocal cascade; a single shrinking transition, even
    with genuine weak index escape, is now rigorously excluded.

134. **Threshold-null closure is topology-sensitive: ordinary-`L2`
    closure is fatal, while the natural reciprocal-theta space has an exact
    Hardy-zero counterexample.**  The ground-coordinate Hilbert spaces are
    `H_u=L2(CK dx)` and `H_f=L2((C/K)dx)`, related unitarily by `f=Ku`.
    Item 45's density of `{p(-D^2)K}` is in ordinary even `L2(dx)` and
    therefore cannot be inserted into a closed-form argument on `H_f`
    without a new topology bridge.

    The failure is exact, not merely a warning about norms.  Let gamma be any
    known real Hardy zero of Xi and define on `H_f`
    `A_gamma f=int_R f(x)cos(gamma x)dx`.  Cauchy--Schwarz gives
    `|A_gamma f|^2`
    ` <=||f||_(H_f)^2 int_R K(x)cos^2(gamma x)/C(x)dx`,
    so `A_gamma` is a nonzero bounded, hence closed, analysis operator.  Its
    Riesz vector is `K(x)cos(gamma x)/C(x)`.  Equivalently, in `H_u` the
    nonzero vector `v_gamma=cos(gamma x)/C(x)` is square integrable.  The
    scalar feature field `Phi_x=cos(gamma x)` has Gram
    `R_gamma(x,y)=cos(gamma x)cos(gamma y)` and exact centering
    `int K(x)Phi_xdx=Khat(gamma)=0`.  For every polynomial p, integration by
    parts against the double-exponential theta tail gives
    `A_gamma[p(-D^2)K]=p(gamma^2)Khat(gamma)=0`.  Thus a perfectly regular
    naturally closed form can annihilate the entire threshold module without
    vanishing.  This rank-one control does not satisfy the far majorant--its
    sign oscillates and its diagonal is bounded--so it is a domain
    counterexample, not a candidate completion.

    There is nevertheless a sharp conditional no-go.  Suppose, in addition
    to the valid positive core split `Q_W=b+r` with `b,r>=0`, that the Gram
    form r has a nonnegative closed extension on ordinary `L2(dx)`.  For
    `f_p=p(-D^2)K` take the smooth cutoffs `f_(p,R)` from item 52.  They
    satisfy `f_(p,R)->f_p` in ordinary `L2` and
    `Q_W[f_(p,R)]->0`.  Positivity gives
    `0<=r[f_(p,R)]<=Q_W[f_(p,R)]->0`; the triangle inequality for the form
    seminorm and ordinary-`L2` closedness then put every `f_p` in `ker r`.
    The kernel of a closed nonnegative form is ordinary-`L2` closed, so item
    45's global even density forces every even `L2` function into `ker r`.

    On compactly supported smooth functions the Gram representation now
    yields `int phi(x)Phi_xdx=0` for every even test phi.  Bochner centering
    makes Phi locally integrable because K is bounded below on compact
    intervals; hence `Phi_(-x)=-Phi_x` for almost every x.  For almost every
    `x>D/2` this gives
    `R(x,-x)=-||Phi_x||^2<=0`, contradicting the required pointwise bound
    `R(x,-x)>=q(2x)>0`.  Consequently every ordinary-`L2`-closable regular
    Gram completion is impossible.

    What remains is now precise.  A logarithmic cascade must be closed, if at
    all, only in the natural reciprocal-theta form topology and must be
    singular or unbounded from the ordinary-`L2` viewpoint.  It must also
    escape the explicit real-zero spectral annihilator, whose bounded
    oscillatory features cannot meet the far sign or size.  Proving weighted
    density of the threshold module is impossible as stated because
    `v_gamma` is an unconditional nonzero annihilator; any further bridge
    must use the pointwise far majorant together with the full ultraviolet
    cascade, not closure or threshold nullity alone.

135. **A single tail cut closes every literal Bochner-centered pointwise
    Gram completion, including the all-log weak-index cascade.**  Retain
    `D=log p`, `R(x,y)=<Phi_x,Phi_y>`, the pointwise bound
    `R(x,y)>=q(|x-y|)`, and the literal Bochner normalization
    `int_R K(x)Phi_xdx=0`.  For `E_X=[X,infinity)` put
    `V_X=int_(E_X)K(x)Phi_xdx`.  Bochner integrability gives the absolute
    Fubini bound
    `iint_(E_X times E_X^c)K(x)K(y)|R(x,y)|dxdy`
    ` <=[int_(E_X)K||Phi||][int_(E_X^c)K||Phi||]<infinity`,
    and exact centering therefore gives
    `iint_(E_X times E_X^c)K(x)K(y)R(x,y)dxdy`
    `=<V_X,-V_X>=-||V_X||^2<=0`.                         `(135.1)`

    The corresponding q-integral is absolutely convergent.  The pole part
    `2cosh((x-y)/2)` factors into finite exponential moments of K.  Away from
    the cut boundary J decays exponentially, while at the boundary
    `J(t)<1/t`; in coordinates `t=x-y>0`, the allowed x-interval has length
    t, which cancels this singularity.  Hence the pointwise majorant can be
    integrated and would imply that the left side of (135.1) is at least
    `I_q(X)=iint_(E_X times E_X^c)K(x)K(y)q(x-y)dxdy`.

    This last integral is strictly positive for all sufficiently large X.
    Indeed, on `y in [-1,0]` and `x>=X>=log 2`, item 81 gives
    `q(x-y)>=(5/6)exp((x-y)/2)`.  The exact positive theta expansion and its
    `n=1` tail asymptotic imply that for one fixed `c_K>0`,
    `K(X+s)>=c_KK(X)` whenever `0<=s<=exp(-2X)` and X is large.  Thus the
    indicated central rectangle contributes at least
    `P_X>=c_+K(X)exp(-3X/2)`, where
    `c_+=(5c_K/6)int_(-1)^0K(y)exp(-y/2)dy>0`.             `(135.2)`

    Only the boundary wedge `0<t=x-y<D` can contribute negatively.  Write
    `x=X+s`, so `0<s<t<D`.  Since `-q(t)<J(t)<1/t` and K is decreasing on
    the positive tail,
    `N_X:=iint_(E_X times E_X^c)K(x)K(y)[-q(x-y)]_+dxdy`
    ` <=K(X-D)int_0^D K(X+s)log(D/s)ds`.                  `(135.3)`
    This bound also proves the claimed local absolute integrability.  If
    `y_n=pi n^2exp(2x)`, every positive theta summand has
    `-k_n'(x)/k_n(x)=2y_n-5/2-4y_n/(2y_n-3)>=y_n`
    for all sufficiently large x.  Consequently
    `K(X+s)<=K(X)exp[-pi exp(2X)s]`.  With
    `A=pi exp(2X)` and
    `int_0^(AD)exp(-u)log(AD/u)du<=log(AD)+2`, (135.3) gives the explicit
    asymptotic bound
    `N_X<=[K(X-D)K(X)/A][log(AD)+2]`
    `    =O(XK(X-D)K(X)exp(-2X))`.                        `(135.4)`

    Dividing (135.4) by (135.2) gives
    `N_X/P_X=O(XK(X-D)exp(-X/2))->0`.  All other cross pairs have
    separation at least D and hence nonnegative q.  Therefore
    `I_q(X)>=P_X-N_X>0` for every sufficiently large X.  Integrating
    `R>=q` now turns (135.1) into the contradiction
    `-||V_X||^2>=I_q(X)>0`.

    No prime atom, diagonal value, operator domain, or finite-to-infinite
    inference enters this cut argument: q is precisely the ordinary
    off-diagonal majorant, and its singularity is integrable across the cut.
    Thus items 127--134's entire literal-Bochner covariance remainder is
    closed, including arbitrary multiscale transition widths and weak channel
    escape.  This does not contradict the formal choice `R=Q_W` under RH,
    because the Weil kernel contains prime-line and ultraviolet
    distributions and is not a locally Bochner-integrable pointwise feature
    field.  Any remaining covariance route must therefore use a genuinely
    non-Bochner/distributional normalization and must verify the full common
    form domain independently.

136. **Every state-independent zero-floor shortcut norm with the item-94
    density property has a remote exposed-face obstruction.**  Put
    `ell=log 2`.  Let d be a separating reflection-invariant metric with
    `d<=d_U`, finite `pi`-second moment, and bounded d-Lipschitz functions
    dense in `L2(pi)`.  Suppose a state-independent dyadic shortcut norm has
    a zero-floor sequence `N_k->infinity`: direct shortcut edges give
    `epsilon_k:=sup_x d(x,x+N_k ell)->0`.  No compact completion, character,
    or finite-dimensional feature representation is assumed.

    First fix any bounded exact contact f at any finite ordered pair `(a,b)`:
    `Lip_d(f)<=1` and `f(b)-f(a)=D=d(a,b)`.  The orientation-preserving
    remote pair is
    `a_k^+=a-N_k ell`, `b_k^+=b+N_k ell`; the orientation-reversing pair is
    `a_k^-=b-N_k ell`, `b_k^-=a+N_k ell`.  Endpoint comparison gives, for
    either sign,
    `|d(a_k^sign,b_k^sign)-D|<=2epsilon_k`.
    The candidate f at the plus pair and the candidate `-f` at the minus pair
    miss exact saturation by at most `4epsilon_k`.

    This deficit can be removed without a compactness argument.  If h is a
    unit-Lipschitz candidate at an ordered pair `(A,B)`, put
    `D_(A,B)=d(A,B)` and
    `delta=D_(A,B)-[h(B)-h(A)]>=0`.  Then
    `G(x)=max{h(x),h(A)+D_(A,B)-d(x,B)}`
    is unit-Lipschitz, satisfies `G(B)-G(A)=D_(A,B)`, and obeys exactly
    `0<=G-h<=delta`; the last inequality follows from
    `h(x)>=h(B)-d(x,B)`.  Applying this cone repair produces exact remote
    contacts `G_k^+` and `G_k^-` with
    `||G_k^+-f||_infinity<=4epsilon_k` and
    `||G_k^-+f||_infinity<=4epsilon_k`.  Thus every finite exposed contact,
    including contacts on nonprecompact or minimally almost-periodic orbit
    completions, reappears at opposite tails with both reset signs.

    The generator passage is uniform for these contacts.  They are uniformly
    bounded and unit d-Lipschitz, hence locally d_U-Lipschitz.  For a finite
    Levy cutoff, the right difference quotient of the full sharp
    rate-`1/2` contraction inequality can be differentiated at a contact;
    the cutoff is used only to justify the generator limit, not to assert
    contraction for a truncated process.  Weighted PNT and tightness give,
    as `x->+/-infinity`,
    `L F(x)=int F d beta^+-(1/2)F(x)+o(1)` and
    `L F(x)=int F d beta^--(1/2)F(x)+o(1)`, respectively, uniformly under the
    displayed sup-norm perturbations.  Here
    `beta^+(dy)=2exp(-y/2)K(y)dy` and
    `beta^-(dy)=2exp(y/2)K(y)dy`.

    Removing the Levy cutoff is harmless.  On `|y-x|<1`, use
    `J(h)<1/|h|`, the d_U-Lipschitz bound, and the positive theta tail to
    bound the archimedean integral by a constant times
    `C(x)^(-1)K(|x|-1)sup_(|s-|x||<=1)U'(s)`, which tends to zero.  On the
    complement, boundedness and `J(h)=O(exp(-h/2))` give `O(exp(-|x|))`
    plus a double-exponential theta tail.  This also proves absolute
    convergence at every contact.  Outgoing prime mass vanishes, while the
    inward prime measures converge to the displayed beta laws.  Therefore,
    with `sigma=beta^+-beta^-`, the exact endpoint term cancels and
    `L G_k^+(b_k^+)-L G_k^+(a_k^+)
       +(1/2)d(a_k^+,b_k^+) -> int f d sigma`,
    whereas the reversed contacts converge to `-int f d sigma`.
    Sharp contraction would make both left sides nonpositive.  It follows
    that it would force
    `int f d sigma=0`                                      `(136.1)`
    for every bounded exact finite contact f.

    Exact contacts are dense enough to make (136.1) impossible.  Let h be
    any bounded nonconstant d-Lipschitz function and normalize
    `Lip_d(h)=1`.  Choose oriented pairs `(a_j,b_j)` whose Lipschitz ratios
    `q_j=[h(b_j)-h(a_j)]/d(a_j,b_j)` tend to one.  Boundedness gives
    `d(a_j,b_j)<=2||h||_infinity/q_j`; hence the absolute deficits
    `delta_j=d(a_j,b_j)(1-q_j)` tend to zero.  The same cone formula gives
    exact contacts `g_j` with `0<=g_j-h<=delta_j`.  Equation (136.1) and
    finite total variation of sigma imply `int h d sigma=0`.  Constants and
    rescaling show that every bounded d-Lipschitz function annihilates sigma.

    This contradicts the density hypothesis without any metric-kernel
    injectivity assumption.  Since `pi(dx)=4cosh(x/2)K(x)dx`, one has exactly
    `sigma(dx)=-4sinh(x/2)K(x)dx`
    and therefore
    `d sigma/d pi=-tanh(x/2)`, a bounded nonzero member of `L2(pi)`.
    Density would make this nonzero function orthogonal to all of `L2(pi)`.
    Consequently the asymptotic negative-polar condition is false: some
    bounded finite contact has a nonzero reset moment, and the two remote
    cone-repaired sequences make one sign strictly positive.  Equivalently,
    the required `limsup` of remote exposed-face reset moments is positive.

    For comparison, the canonical distance contacts alone yield the weaker
    intrinsic condition `V(z)=int d(x,z)d sigma(x)=0` for every z.  Strong
    negative type would already contradict this; on a compact abelian
    completion it says only `rhohat(chi)sigmahat(chi)=0`, and no injectivity
    may be inferred when `rhohat` has zeros.  The all-contact cone argument
    is what removes that possible non-characteristic escape.  Thus every
    state-independent zero-floor transport metric capable of using item 94
    is closed; a surviving transport construction must lose uniform cheap
    translations or the density needed for the RH implication.

137. **The tail-cut obstruction survives the exact natural closed-form
    domain; only genuine off-diagonal distributions evade it.**  Put
    `H_f=L2((C/K)dx)`, write `f=Ku`, and on the compact smooth core define
    the absolute comparison form
    `a[Ku]=E_K(u)+F_K(u)+||Ku||_(H_f)^2`.  Suppose a proposed item-81
    completion gives closed nonnegative forms b and r on `H_f` such that
    `Q_W=b+r` on that core.  Suppose also that the off-diagonal polarization
    of r is represented, including on finite-energy cuts obtained by form
    closure, by a symmetric Radon density R with
    `R(x,y)>=q(|x-y|)` almost everywhere off the diagonal, and that no
    derivative-of-delta or boundary-supported residue contributes to such a
    cut.  These are precisely the common-domain and ordinary-Gram hypotheses
    which the extended version of the cascade still had to verify.

    The cut vector is automatically in this common domain.  For
    `u_X=1_[X,infinity)` and `f_X=Ku_X`, the pole energy `F_K(u_X)` is finite
    by factorization into the four exponential half-moments of K.  The
    archimedean part of `E_K(u_X)` is the finite J-cross integral bounded in
    item 135.  For the prime part, monotonicity and evenness of K give, with
    `ell=log n`,
    `int_R K(x)K(x+ell)dx<=2||K||_1 K(ell/2)`.             `(137.1)`
    Indeed at least one of `|x|,|x+ell|` is at least `ell/2`, and the other
    factor is integrable.  The theta estimate
    `K(log(n)/2)<=4pi^2 n^(9/4)exp(-pi n)` therefore makes
    `sum_(n>=2) Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)int K(x)K(x+log n)dx`
    finite.  This dominates the prime energy of every bounded cut.

    Smooth compact approximants to `u_X` converge in a.  Truncating the two
    theta tails is controlled by the preceding exponential moments and the
    summable majorant (137.1).  Smoothing a boundary on a window of width
    epsilon costs `O(epsilon log(1/epsilon))` for the local
    `J(h)=O(1/|h|)` kernel and tends to zero for the bounded cosh kernel.
    Every fixed prime translate converges by dominated convergence, and
    (137.1) permits passage through the prime sum.  The same argument with
    both remote boundaries shows that K, corresponding to `u=1`, is in the
    a-closure of the core and has zero `E_K+F_K` energy.

    Positivity of the proposed split yields on the core
    `0<=r[g]<=Q_W[g]=E_K(g/K)-F_K(g/K)<=a[g]`.             `(137.2)`
    Thus the a-approximants are Cauchy in the r-form norm.  Closedness puts
    `f_X`, `K-f_X`, and K in `D(r)`.  Since the compact approximants to K
    have `Q_W` energy tending to zero, (137.2) gives `r[K]=0`.  Form
    Cauchy--Schwarz then gives `r(K,g)=0` for every `g in D(r)`, and hence
    `r(f_X,K-f_X)=-r[f_X]<=0`.                             `(137.3)`

    The Radon/no-residue hypothesis identifies the left side of (137.3)
    with
    `iint_(E_X times E_X^c)K(x)K(y)R(x,y)dxdy`.            `(137.4)`
    This integral is absolute: its negative part is bounded by the
    integrable `q^-` cut wedge from item 135, while finite form polarization
    then forces its positive part to be finite.  Exhausting the cut by
    compact sets and using `R>=q` therefore turns (137.4) into a quantity at
    least `I_q(X)>0` for all sufficiently large X, contradicting (137.3).

    This extension uses neither literal Bochner integrability of a feature
    field nor ordinary-`L2` closure.  It closes every all-log or weak-index
    cascade that actually satisfies the natural item-81 common-domain and
    Radon polarization requirements.  It does not contradict the formal
    choice `r=Q_W`, `b=0` if RH holds: the Weil off-diagonal distribution
    contains negative prime-line atoms, as well as its ultraviolet finite-
    part structure, and is not a Radon density bounded below by q.  Those
    singular cut terms are exactly the remaining possible cancellation.
    Reconstructing them in a positive square is the original
    Weil/Douglas positivity problem, not an independent covariance proof.

138. **The genuine Weil prime lines cancel the tail-cut advantage to first
    order (exact cut polarization and unconditional PNT asymptotic).**  Let
    `E_X=[X,infinity)`, `u_X=1_(E_X)`, and `f_X=K u_X`.  Item 137's
    `E_K+F_K` approximation puts `f_X`, `K-f_X`, and K in the natural Weil
    form domain.  Polarizing the exact item-51 ground-state identity gives,
    with `q(t)=2cosh(t/2)-J(t)`,
    `Q_W(f_X,K-f_X)=I_q(X)-P_X`,                         `(138.1)`
    where
    `I_q(X)=iint_(x>=X>y)K(x)K(y)q(x-y)dydx` and
    `P_X=sum_(n>=2)Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)
       int_(X-log n)^X K(a)K(a+log n)da`.                `(138.2)`
    Thus the Radon part contributes the positive quantity from item 135,
    but every prime line crossing the cut contributes with the opposite
    sign.  Formula (138.1) includes all terms of the full distribution.  In
    fact the archimedean difference square contributes
    `-iint_(x>=X>y)J(x-y)K(x)K(y)`, the pole square contributes the same
    integral with `2cosh((x-y)/2)`, and the prime difference squares
    contribute `-P_X`.  The canonical diagonal finite part has no residual:
    it is exactly the diagonal term absorbed by the K-radical Picone
    identity, and polarization against the complementary constant has zero
    increment.  Equivalently, `Q_W(K,g)=0` follows directly by polarizing
    that identity with the constant function one.

    There is no hidden point-evaluation of the finite part.  Near the cut,
    `J(x-y)=O(1/(x-y))` and
    `int_0^delta int_0^delta (s+t)^(-1)dsdt<infinity`.
    Smooth transitions of width epsilon converge with extra local cost
    `O(epsilon log(1/epsilon))`; theta-tail truncation and the prime sum are
    dominated by (137.1).  Hence (138.1) is the limit of compact smooth-core
    polarizations, not a formal product of distributions.

    Put
    `T_X^+=int_X^infinity exp(x/2)K(x)dx` and
    `T_X^-=int_X^infinity exp(-x/2)K(x)dx`.
    Termwise integration of the positive theta expansion is exact:
    `T_X^+=pi exp(3X)sum_(m>=1)m^2
       exp[-pi m^2exp(2X)]`.                             `(138.3)`
    Indeed, for `z=pi m^2exp(2x)`, the m-th integrand becomes
    `(2sqrt(pi)m)^(-1)z^(1/2)(2z-3)exp(-z)dz`, the negative
    derivative of `z^(3/2)exp(-z)/(sqrt(pi)m)`.  Since
    `int exp(+/-x/2)K(x)dx=1/4`, the pole cross term is exactly
    `I_pole(X)=(T_X^++T_X^-)/4-2T_X^+T_X^-`.             `(138.4)`

    The J cross term is `o(T_X^+)`.  Fix `D=log 2`.  On `x-y>=D`,
    `J(x-y)<=C_D exp(-(x-y)/2)`, so this part is at most
    `C_D T_X^- int_(-infinity)^X exp(y/2)K(y)dy
       <=(C_D/4)exp(-X)T_X^+`.
    In the boundary wedge, write `x=X+s`, `0<s<t=x-y<D`.  Exactly as in
    item 135,
    `H_J^near(X)<=K(X-D)int_0^D K(X+s)log(D/s)ds
       <=[K(X-D)K(X)/(pi exp(2X))]
          [log(pi D exp(2X))+2]`.                        `(138.5)`
    The theta series and (138.3) give, for all sufficiently large X,
    `K(X)/T_X^+<=4pi exp(3X/2)`.  Dividing (138.5) by `T_X^+` leaves
    `O(X K(X-D)exp(-X/2))=o(1)`.  Therefore
    `I_q(X)=(1/4+o(1))T_X^+`.                            `(138.6)`

    The prime term has exactly the same main term.  Define the full incoming
    prime convolution
    `H(r)=sum_(n>=2)Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)K(r-log n)`.
    Ordinary PNT, and no zero-density or RH-strength estimate, gives
    `exp(-r/2)H(r)->int exp(-y/2)K(y)dy=1/4`,             `(138.7)`
    and also `H(r)<=C exp(r/2)`.  For completeness, set
    `W(y)=exp(y/2)K(y)` and
    `nu_r=e^(-r)sum_n Lambda(n)delta_(r-log n)`.  PNT gives
    `nu_r([a,b])->int_a^b exp(-y)dy` on every compact interval.  Chebyshev's
    bound gives uniformly
    `nu_r([j,j+1])<=C exp(-j)`, while
    `sum_j exp(-j)sup_[j,j+1]W<infinity` by the two theta tails.  Dominated
    compact convergence proves (138.7) and its displayed global bound.

    Changing variables `r=a+log n` in (138.2) and using positivity gives
    `P_X=int_X^infinity K(r)H_X(r)dr`,
    `H_X(r)=sum_(log n>=r-X)Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)K(r-log n)`.
    For `X<=r<X+log 2`, `H_X(r)=H(r)`; afterwards `0<=H_X(r)<=H(r)`.
    Consequently (138.7) yields
    `P_X=(1/4+o(1))int_X^(X+log2)exp(r/2)K(r)dr
          +O(T_(X+log2)^+)`.
    Formula (138.3) gives
    `T_(X+log2)^+/T_X^+=O(exp[-3pi exp(2X)])`, and hence
    `P_X=(1/4+o(1))T_X^+`.                               `(138.8)`

    Combining (138.1), (138.6), and (138.8),
    `P_X/I_q(X)->1` and
    `Q_W(f_X,K-f_X)=o(I_q(X))`.                          `(138.9)`
    This is an unconditional asymptotic falsifier for extending the item-135
    cut argument through the actual Weil distribution: its negative prime
    atoms necessarily cancel the positive Radon tail cut at leading order.
    Their smaller signed remainder is a weighted PNT-error functional, so
    assigning it a favorable sign would be new arithmetic input, not a
    consequence of centering or positive-semidefinite cut geometry.

139. **A state-dependent zero-cost sequence cannot touch a compact set or
    join opposite tails; only same-tail reciprocal-theta localization
    remains.**  Let d be an intrinsic shortcut metric satisfying the item-94
    hypotheses, with `d<=d_U`, and give the prime-power edge
    `z<->z+log q` an arbitrary positive assigned cost `a_q(z)`.  Suppose
    there are edges `(x_n,y_n)` with assigned costs tending to zero and with
    one endpoint in a fixed compact set.  Their intrinsic lengths
    `D_n=d(x_n,y_n)` also tend to zero.  After a subsequence the compact
    endpoint tends to some finite a.  If the prime powers q_n were bounded,
    discreteness would make one q repeat; continuity from `d<=d_U` would then
    give `d(a,a+/-log q)=0`, contrary to separation.  Hence the other
    endpoint tends to `+infinity` or `-infinity`.

    Assume first that `A_n->a` is the compact endpoint and `B_n->+infinity`
    is the remote endpoint; the other orientation and the negative tail are
    identical.  For any bounded unit d-Lipschitz h, both h and `-h` are
    within absolute contact deficit `2D_n` at `(A_n,B_n)`.  More precisely,
    for `s=+/-1` put
    `delta_(n,s)=D_n-[s h(B_n)-s h(A_n)]`, so
    `0<=delta_(n,s)<=2D_n`, and set
    `G_(n,s)(x)=max{s h(x),s h(A_n)+D_n-d(x,B_n)}`.
    Then `G_(n,s)` is an exact unit contact and
    `0<=G_(n,s)-s h<=delta_(n,s)`.

    The generator is continuous under these particular cone repairs despite
    `J(t)~1/(2|t|)`.  On a compact neighborhood of a, the correction has
    sup norm at most delta and d_U-Lipschitz constant at most a fixed L.
    Its local archimedean contribution is bounded by
    `C int_0^eta min{Lt,2delta}dt/t`
    `=O(delta+delta log(eta/delta))->0`; outside that neighborhood the jump
    rate is finite and the sup-norm bound applies.  The prime rates converge
    locally by their summable theta majorant.  Thus
    `L G_(n,s)(A_n)->s Lh(a)`.

    At the remote endpoint, split the Levy integral in the same way.  The
    local coefficient is bounded by
    `C(B_n)^(-1)K(B_n-1)sup_[B_n-1,B_n+1]U'->0`; outside it, the sup-norm
    perturbation and theta tightness apply.  Weighted PNT gives the inward
    reset law, while `|h(B_n)-h(a)|<=D_n+o(1)`.  Consequently
    `L G_(n,s)(B_n)->s[int h d beta^+-(1/2)h(a)]`.
    Differentiating the full sharp contraction inequality at each exact
    contact, using a finite Levy cutoff only to justify the difference
    quotient and then removing it by the displayed bounds, forces both a
    quantity and its negative to be nonpositive.  Therefore
    `Lh(a)=int h d beta^+-(1/2)h(a)`                    `(139.1)`
    for every bounded d-Lipschitz h.  A negative remote endpoint gives the
    same identity with `beta^-`.

    Identity (139.1) is impossible at a finite state.  Write the actual jump
    generator as
    `Lh(a)=int[h(y)-h(a)]Q_a(dy)`.  Since each beta law has mass `1/2`,
    (139.1) says that this integral equals
    `int[h(y)-h(a)]beta^+(dy)` for every bounded Lipschitz h.  But Q_a has a
    positive q=2 atom at `y_0=a+log 2`, of mass
    `w_2=c_2K(a+log 2)/C(a)>0`, whereas beta^+ is nonatomic.  Separation
    gives `r=d(a,y_0)>0`.  For `0<s<r/3` take the bounded unit-Lipschitz test
    `phi_s(y)=(s-d(y,y_0))_+`; it vanishes at a.  Dividing the asserted
    equality by s gives a left side at least w_2, while the right side is at
    most `beta^+(B_d(y_0,s))`, which tends to
    `beta^+({y_0})=0`.  The d-ball excludes an ordinary neighborhood of a
    because `d<=d_U`, so the Levy integral there is finite.  This is a strict
    contradiction.  The beta^- case is identical.

    Cheap edges joining the two opposite tails are also impossible.  Apply
    the same two cone repairs to `x_n->-infinity`, `y_n->+infinity`.
    The endpoint value difference tends to zero because `D_n->0`, and the
    two tail formulas leave exactly `+/-int h d(beta^+-beta^-)`.  Sharp
    contraction would make this vanish for every bounded d-Lipschitz h.
    Item 94's density and
    `d(beta^+-beta^-)/d pi=-tanh(x/2)` give the same contradiction as in
    item 136.

    There is a real limitation: positive incident cost on each fixed compact
    set does not by itself let item 122 be localized.  If a U-bump has height
    and U-width eta, the compensated J singularity contains a term of size
    `(K(x)/C(x))eta log(1/eta)` before contact normalization; in the
    derivative inequality the associated local-rate loss is of order
    `(K(x)U'(x)/C(x))log(1/eta)`.  A local floor may decay faster than the
    reciprocal-theta scale needed to keep this below item 122's fixed gain.
    One therefore cannot choose `eta` below an arbitrary moving local floor
    and cite the positive-floor theorem.  The exact survivor is now narrow:
    every sequence of vanishing costs must have both endpoints escape into
    the same tail, and the incident floors in all usable tail windows must be
    reciprocal-theta-small or smaller.  On such a same-tail edge the common
    reset law cancels and the holding term contracts its endpoint difference
    at exactly rate `1/2`, so the preceding exposed-face argument has no sign
    left to exploit.

140. **Prime-line cancellation holds to every algebraic tail order; its
    residual is a genuine beyond-all-orders term.**  Define
    `w(r)=2r^(-1/2)K(log r)` and
    `a(r)=2pi r^2(2pi r^2-3)exp(-pi r^2)`.  The positive theta expansion gives
    the exact orbit decomposition `w(r)=sum_(m>=1)a(mr)`.  Absolute
    convergence and the divisor identity `sum_(d|n)Lambda(d)=log n` then
    give, for `Y>0`,
    `S_w(Y):=sum_n Lambda(n)w(n/Y)=sum_(m>=1)(log m)a(m/Y)`. `(140.1)`
    Thus no prime-number-theorem error is hidden in the following expansion.

    The Mellin transform of the single orbit is
    `A(s)=int_0^infinity a(r)r^(s-1)dr`
    `    =[s(s-1)/2]pi^(-s/2)Gamma(s/2)=xi(s)/zeta(s)`.   `(140.2)`
    For `c>1`, Mellin inversion of (140.1) is therefore
    `S_w(Y)=(2pi i)^(-1)int_(c-i infinity)^(c+i infinity)
                 [-A(s)zeta'(s)]Y^s ds`.                 `(140.3)`
    Fix `N>=1` and shift to `sigma_N=-(2N+1)`.  Stirling's formula gives
    exponential decay `exp(-pi|t|/4)` from the gamma factor, while the zeta
    functional equation gives only polynomial-logarithmic growth for
    `zeta'(sigma_N+it)`.  The new vertical integral is absolutely convergent,
    the horizontal integrals vanish, and uniformly for `Y>=1` its absolute
    value is at most `C_NY^(-(2N+1))`.  The pole at one has residue `Y/2`.
    At `s=-2k`, the gamma residue together with
    `zeta'(-2k)=(-1)^k(2k)!zeta(2k+1)/[2(2pi)^(2k)]`
    gives residue `-xi(2k+1)Y^(-2k)`.  Hence the exact finite-N formula is
    `S_w(Y)=Y/2-sum_(k=1)^N xi(2k+1)Y^(-2k)+R_N(Y)`,
    `|R_N(Y)|<=C_NY^(-(2N+1))`.                           `(140.4)`

    Put `D(x)=sum_n Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)K(x-log n)`.  From the definition of w,
    `D(x)=S_w(exp x)/(2exp(x/2))`.  If
    `alpha_k=2k+1/2` and
    `M_alpha=int_R K(y)exp(alpha y)dy=xi(alpha+1/2)/2`, (140.4) becomes,
    uniformly for `x>=X>=0`,
    `D(x)=exp(x/2)/4-sum_(k=1)^N M_(alpha_k)exp(-alpha_k x)
            +O_N(exp(-(2N+3/2)x))`.                      `(140.5)`
    In particular `M_(5/2)=xi(3)/2=3zeta(3)/(4pi)`, the coefficient found
    independently in item 122.

    These are exactly the coefficients of the ordinary cut.  Write
    `A_beta(X)=int_X^infinity K(x)exp(beta x)dx`.  Since
    `J(t)=sum_(k>=0)exp[-(2k+1/2)t]` for `t>0`, Tonelli gives the exact
    J-cross integral
    `I_J(X)=sum_(k>=0)A_(-alpha_k)(X)
                 [M_(alpha_k)-A_(alpha_k)(X)]`,           `(140.6)`
    where now `alpha_0=1/2`.  Also `M_(1/2)=1/4`, and item 138's factored
    pole integral is
    `(A_(1/2)+A_(-1/2))/4-2A_(1/2)A_(-1/2)`.  Splitting the tail of (140.6)
    at N consequently yields
    `I_q(X)=A_(1/2)(X)/4
             -sum_(k=1)^N M_(alpha_k)A_(-alpha_k)(X)
             +O_N(A_(-(2N+5/2))(X))`.                    `(140.7)`
    Here is the required uniform error audit.  For the J-series remainder,
    on `t>=1` use
    `sum_(k>N)exp(-alpha_k t)<=C_Nexp(-(2N+5/2)t)` and factor the two
    exponential moments.  On `0<t<1`, use `J(t)<1/t` and the item-135
    boundary-wedge bound
    `O(XK(X-1)K(X)exp(-2X))`.  The positive theta tail and its fixed-width
    lower bound for `A_(-beta)` make this smaller than
    `A_(-(2N+5/2))` for fixed N.  Every finite tail-tail product
    `A_alpha A_(-alpha)` obeys the same bound, after increasing the constant,
    because K is double exponentially small.

    The crossing prime mass has the exact representation
    `P_X=int_X^infinity K(x)D(x)dx-E_X`,
    `E_X=sum_n Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)
           int_(X+log n)^infinity K(x)K(x-log n)dx>=0`.   `(140.8)`
    The global bound `D(x)<=Cexp(x/2)` from item 138 gives
    `E_X<=C A_(1/2)(X+log 2)`.  Formula (138.3) shows that this is smaller
    than `A_(-B)(X)` for every fixed B.  Integrating (140.5) in (140.8)
    therefore gives
    `P_X=A_(1/2)(X)/4
             -sum_(k=1)^N M_(alpha_k)A_(-alpha_k)(X)
             +O_N(A_(-(2N+3/2))(X))`.                    `(140.9)`
    Comparison with (140.7), item 138's `I_q(X)>=cA_(1/2)(X)`, and the
    elementary bound
    `A_(-B)(X)<=exp(-(B+1/2)X)A_(1/2)(X)` prove
    `|P_X-I_q(X)|/I_q(X)<=C_Nexp(-(2N+2)X)`.              `(140.10)`
    Since N is arbitrary, for every fixed L there is `C_L` such that this
    ratio is at most `C_Lexp(-LX)`.  This is an unconditional
    superalgebraic cancellation, not an assertion that the residual is zero
    or has either sign.  In every use above N is fixed before `X->infinity`;
    the constants are not uniform in N, so optimal truncation and a
