    Stokes-scale sign do not follow.

    Finitely shifted cuts do not reveal an algebraic sign.  For fixed
    positive shifts the leftmost nonzero boundary dominates all later ones
    by a double-theta factor.  For shifts on the natural `exp(-2X)` tail
    scale, a finite signed step packet of width below `log 2` contains no
    internal prime edge.  Its prime energy has pointwise coefficient
    `D(x)` (the outgoing coefficient is double-theta-small), while its pole
    and far-J coefficients are exactly the moment expansion in (140.5)--
    (140.7); local tail-tail terms are again beyond every fixed algebraic
    order.  Thus the same coefficient matching survives every such finite
    shifted packet.  Moreover subtracting two scalar cut inequalities is
    not itself a positive-semidefinite cut identity.  Determining the sign
    beyond (140.10) requires a uniform Stokes-scale remainder or an exact
    positive factorization; finite residue asymptotics cannot provide it.

141. **The beyond-all-orders cut residual is eventually positive
    unconditionally; it is a high-zero boundary term, not an RH detector.**
    Retain the notation of items 138 and 140 and put
    `f_X=K 1_[X,infinity)` and
    `R_X=P_X-I_q(X)`.  The step closure proved in item 137 permits the
    item-51 Picone identity to be used without a pointwise finite-part
    convention.  For this step, its archimedean jump energy is exactly the
    J-cross integral, its prime jump energy is `P_X`, and its pole energy is
    the `2cosh` cross integral.  Consequently
    `R_X=Q_W(f_X)`.                                      `(141.1)`
    This fixes the sign: item 138's complementary polarization is
    `Q_W(f_X,K-f_X)=-Q_W(f_X)=I_q-P_X`, since K is in the
    unconditional Weil radical.

    Here is an exact contour representation of the Stokes residual.  For
    `s in C` define the entire tail transform
    `A_s(X)=int_X^infinity K(x)exp(sx)dx`.  If rho is a nontrivial zeta zero,
    set `s_rho=rho-1/2`.  In the centered Fourier convention used in item 42,
    a Xi zero `z_rho` satisfies `i z_rho=s_rho`; for real `f_X`,
    `fhat_X(z_rho) overline(fhat_X(conj(z_rho)))
       =A_(s_rho)(X)A_(-s_rho)(X)`.
    The Weil zero side and (141.1) therefore give, with every zero repeated
    according to multiplicity and with symmetric height summation,
    `R_X=sum_rho A_(s_rho)(X)A_(-s_rho)(X)`.             `(141.2)`
    This sum is in fact absolute.  One direct justification of the passage
    from the smooth core is to smooth the single jump monotonically and
    truncate the theta tail.  The resulting transforms have, uniformly on
    `|Re(s)|<=1/2`, a `C_X/(1+|Im(s)|)` bound by integration by parts and
    uniformly bounded total variation.  The zero count `N(T)=O(T log T)`
    then gives an absolute `C_X/(1+|Im(s)|^2)` product majorant.  The prime,
    archimedean, and pole sides converge by the item-137 form estimates.

    Equivalently, if `c>1`, put
    `H_X(s)=A_(s-1/2)(X)A_(1/2-s)(X)`.  It is entire and
    `H_X(1-s)=H_X(s)`.  A rectangular contour with vertical sides c and
    `1-c`, at heights avoiding zeros, has vanishing horizontal sides:
    integration by parts gives `H_X(s)=O_X(|Im(s)|^(-2))`, whereas the
    logarithmic derivative has polynomial-logarithmic growth.  Since
    `xi(1-s)=xi(s)`, the two vertical integrals are negatives of one another.
    The only enclosed poles of `xi'/xi` are the nontrivial zeros.  Thus
    `(141.2)` is also the exact Mellin contour
    `R_X=(pi i)^(-1)int_(c-i infinity)^(c+i infinity)
             [xi'(s)/xi(s)]H_X(s)ds`.                   `(141.3)`
    The residue at rho is precisely its multiplicity times
    `A_(s_rho)A_(-s_rho)`.  There are no pole or trivial-zero residues in
    (141.3), because the completed xi is entire and nonzero at zero and one.
    In contrast, the item-140 contour for the incoming prime degree has only
    the pole `s=1`, of residue `Y/2`, and the gamma poles `s=-2k`, of residues
    `-xi(2k+1)Y^(-2k)`.  Hence (141.3) is exactly the term left after every
    residue in the all-orders prime/ordinary-kernel matching has cancelled.

    The sign of (141.2) can nevertheless be determined without RH.  Put
    `U(x)=-K'(x)/K(x)` and `L=U(X)`.  The positive theta expansion gives,
    with `Y=pi exp(2X)`,
    `K(X)=pi exp(5X/2)(2Y-3)exp(-Y)[1+O(Y exp(-3Y))]`,
    `L=2Y-9/2-6/(2Y-3)+O(Y exp(-3Y))`.                  `(141.4)`
    The same termwise estimates, including one derivative, show that U is
    increasing on `[X,infinity)` once X is large.  These assertions are
    uniform there because every `m>=2` theta term and its first two
    derivatives are bounded relative to the `m=1` term by a polynomial in Y
    times `exp[-(m^2-1)Y]`.

    Define
    `B_X(s)=exp(-sX)A_s(X)/K(X)` and
    `e_X(s)=int_0^infinity exp(st)[U(X+t)-L]
                         K(X+t)/K(X)dt`.
    Integration of the derivative of
    `exp(st)K(X+t)/K(X)` is exact and gives
    `(L-s)B_X(s)=1-e_X(s)`.                              `(141.5)`
    For `|Re(s)|<=1/2`, monotonicity of U yields
    `|e_X(s)|<=e_X(1/2)`.  The latter error is explicit, rather than a
    nonuniform Laplace assertion.  Formula (138.3) and the theta formula for
    K give
    `B_X(1/2)=
       [sum_(m>=1)m^2 exp(-m^2Y)]/
       [sum_(m>=1)m^2(2m^2Y-3)exp(-m^2Y)]
       =(2Y-3)^(-1)[1+O(Y exp(-3Y))]`.
    Substitution in (141.5), together with (141.4), gives
    `e_X(1/2)=2/(2Y-3)+6/(2Y-3)^2+O(Y exp(-3Y))=O(1/L)`. `(141.6)`
    Therefore (141.5) holds uniformly for every point of the whole closed
    critical strip, even when `|Im(s)|` tends to infinity, and
    `A_s(X)A_(-s)(X)
       =[K(X)^2/(L^2-s^2)] [1+O(1/L)]`,                 `(141.7)`
    where the error is uniform for `|Re(s)|<=1/2`.

    The uniform error can be summed absolutely.  Write
    `s_rho=eta+i gamma`; then `|eta|<1/2` and, for `L>=1`,
    `|L^2-s_rho^2|>=L^2+gamma^2-1/4`.
    Dyadic partial summation with `N(T)=O(T log T)` gives
    `sum_rho |L^2-s_rho^2|^(-1)=O(log L/L)`.             `(141.8)`
    No zero-density estimate or zero-free strengthening is used here.

    Finally let `G(z)=xi(1/2+z)`.  It is even of order one, and
    `sum_rho |s_rho|^(-2)<infinity`; pairing the zeros `s_rho` and
    `-s_rho` in its Hadamard product gives exactly
    `sum_rho 1/(L^2-s_rho^2)=G'(L)/[L G(L)]
       =[xi'(1/2+L)/xi(1/2+L)]/L`.                      `(141.9)`
    Both sides count all zeros with multiplicity.  From the completed
    definition
    `xi(s)=(1/2)s(s-1)pi^(-s/2)Gamma(s/2)zeta(s)`,
    `xi'(s)/xi(s)=1/s+1/(s-1)-(1/2)log pi
       +(1/2)psi(s/2)+zeta'(s)/zeta(s)`.
    Stirling's formula for psi and the absolutely convergent Dirichlet series
    for `zeta'/zeta` at `s=1/2+L` show
    `xi'(1/2+L)/xi(1/2+L)
       =(1/2)log[L/(2pi)]+O(1/L)`.                      `(141.10)`
    Combining (141.2), (141.7)--(141.10) proves the unconditional asymptotic
    `R_X=K(X)^2 {log[L/(2pi)]/(2L)
                    +O(log L/L^2)}>0`                  `(141.11)`
    for every sufficiently large X.  In particular, eventual nonnegativity
    of this one-cut residual is provable from the functional equation,
    theta tail, and ordinary zero count; it neither assumes nor proves that
    the individual zeros have real centered ordinates.

    Finite shifted-cut cancellation does not evade this conclusion.  For
    distinct fixed shifts `h_1<...<h_m`, (138.3) gives
    `A_(1/2)(X+h_j)/A_(1/2)(X+h_1)->0` for every `j>1`.
    Since both `P_X` and `I_q(X)` are asymptotic to
    `A_(1/2)(X)/4`, the leftmost nonzero coefficient in a fixed signed
    combination dominates; cancellation of its prime leading term forces
    that coefficient to vanish, and induction makes the whole combination
    zero.  There is an equally exact no-go on the natural reciprocal-theta
    scale.  Put `Y=pi exp(2X)` and `h_j=tau_j/Y`, with distinct fixed
    nonnegative tau_j.  The `m=1` term in the exact formula (138.3) has ratio
    `A_(1/2)(X+h_j)/A_(1/2)(X)
       =exp(3tau_j/Y-Y[exp(2tau_j/Y)-1])
          +O(exp(-3Y))`.
    With `z=1/Y`, its algebraic germ is the entire function
    `r_tau(z)=exp(3tau z-[exp(2tau z)-1]/z)`, where the quotient has its
    removable value `2tau` at zero.  If a finite combination cancelled
    every algebraic endpoint coefficient, `sum_j c_j r_(tau_j)(z)` would
    have a zero of infinite order at zero and hence vanish identically.
    Along positive `z->infinity`, the smallest tau term dominates every
    larger one (with `tau=0` handled first); division and induction force
    every `c_j=0`.  For compactly smoothed packets the analogous statement
    is the elementary moment theorem: vanishing of every endpoint moment
    makes the profile's entire Laplace transform have a zero of infinite
    order, hence makes the profile zero.
    Thus no nonzero finite packet cancels all prime-leading and trivial-zero
    orders while retaining a strictly positive ordinary q term.

    There is also a precise circularity boundary.  Requiring
    `Q_W(sum_j c_j K 1_[X_j,infinity))>=0` for every finite coefficient and
    cut system is matrix positivity, not a consequence of (141.11).
    Smoothed finite step packets are form-dense in `K C_c^infinity`: Riemann
    sums for
    `u(x)=int_(-infinity)^infinity u'(t)1_[t,infinity)(x)dt` converge in the
    item-51 jump and pole energies by the same local-J and prime majorants
    used in item 137.
    Hence positivity for all such packets extends to the item-51 core and is
    exactly Weil positivity, therefore RH-equivalent.  No coefficient choice
    whose required sign invokes that all-packet matrix statement is an
    independent proof mechanism.

142. **Every smooth global tail-hub/star metric fails sharp curvature: a
    compact prime atom forces the radius back into the item-122 regime.**
    Let r be a positive continuous function satisfying
    `|r(x)-r(y)|<=d_U(x,y)` and `r(x)->0` as `x->+infinity`, and put
    `d(x,y)=min{d_U(x,y),r(x)+r(y)}` for `x!=y`.  This is the global
    shortest-path metric obtained by adjoining one hub with edge length
    r(x) at x.  Indeed the Lipschitz condition on r makes every line-to-hub
    detour at least r(x), so no mixed line/hub path improves the displayed
    minimum.  Assume the standard item-94 separation, moment, and density
    hypotheses.  No condition on how the negative tail is glued to the rest
    of the metric is used below.

    The metric completion has a positive-tail point h with `d(z,h)=r(z)`.
    This notation does not assume a Markov extension.  For every bounded
    d-Lipschitz f, the established weighted-PNT tail limit gives directly
    along finite states `R->+infinity`
    `Lf(R)->L_hf:=int_R[f(y)-f(h)]beta^+(dy)`,            `(142.1)`
    because `beta^+` has mass `1/2` and `f(R)->f(h)`.  All uses of h below
    are limits of exact contacts at the finite pair `(x,R)`.

    Fix x, put `D=r(x)`, and let phi be nonnegative, bounded, d-Lipschitz,
    compactly supported, and satisfy `phi(x)=phi(h)=0` and
    `r(z)>=D+phi(z)+delta` on its support for some `delta>0`.  With
    `D_R=d(x,R)`, form the two exact cone repairs
    `G_R^+ =max{ phi,D_R-d(.,R)}` and
    `G_R^- =max{-phi,D_R-d(.,R)}`.  They are unit d-Lipschitz and satisfy
    `G_R^+/- (R)-G_R^+/-(x)=D_R`.  On compact sets, both corrections relative
    to `+/-phi` converge to the same function
    `H_D(z)=(D-r(z))_+`, with `H_D(x)=0` and boundary value `H_D(h)=D`.
    The support margin is exactly what makes the correction vanish for both
    signs on `supp(phi)`.

    Differentiate the alleged rate-`1/2` contraction inequality at these
    finite contacts, using the same finite-Levy cutoff and removal as in
    items 136 and 139.  The functions are uniformly bounded and d-Lipschitz;
    local J increments and the summable prime majorant give convergence at x,
    while (142.1) gives convergence at R.  For the plus and minus repairs one
    obtains respectively
    ` L_h phi-Lphi(x)<=LH_D(x)-int H_D d beta^+`,
    `-L_h phi+Lphi(x)<=LH_D(x)-int H_D d beta^+`.         `(142.2)`
    Thus the right side is nonnegative and
    `|Lphi(x)-L_hphi|<=LH_D(x)-int H_Dd beta^+<=LH_D(x)`. `(142.3)`
    This is the exact boundary-edge inequality; no formal generator was
    inserted at h.

    Test (142.3) on an actual incoming prime atom.  Let p tend through the
    primes and put `x=log p`.  The q=p left-jump atom from x to zero has the
    exact mass
    `omega_p=[log p/sqrt p]K(0)/C(log p)
             =2K(0)log p/(p+1)`.                        `(142.4)`
    In contrast, `beta^+(dy)=2exp(-y/2)K(y)dy` is nonatomic.  For small s set
    `phi_s(z)=(s-d(z,0))_+`.  If `s<r(0)`, its d-ball cannot use the hub and
    is exactly the U-ball `|U(z)-U(0)|<s`.  Since `U'>18` and the beta density
    is bounded near zero, one fixed B gives
    `int phi_s d beta^+<=B s^2`, while positivity of every jump term and
    (142.4) give `Lphi_s(x)>=omega_p s`.  Choose
    `s=omega_p/(2B)` after enlarging B.  Then
    `Lphi_s(x)-L_hphi_s>=c omega_p^2>=c_0 x^2exp(-2x)`.   `(142.5)`

    The support condition used in (142.2) is valid in the only case needed
    for contradiction.  If `D=r(x)<exp(-3x)`, both D and s tend to zero,
    whereas r has a positive minimum on the shrinking compact support of
    `phi_s`; hence `r>=D+phi_s+delta_p` there for some `delta_p>0`.

    There is a uniform elementary upper bound for the other side.  The
    function `H_D` is bounded by D, is unit d-Lipschitz, and vanishes at x.
    The total prime degree and the archimedean rate outside `|y-x|<1` are
    bounded on the positive tail, so those parts of `LH_D(x)` are `O(D)`.
    On the local window let
    `M_x=sup_(|t|<=1)U'(x+t)`.  Then
    `H_D(x+t)<=min{D,M_x|t|}`, `J(|t|)<1/|t|`, and
    `K(x+t)<=K(x-1)`.  Hence the local part is at most
    `[2K(x-1)/C(x)]D[1+log(M_x/D)]`.  The theta tail gives
    `K(x-1)/C(x)<1` and `log M_x=O(x)`.  Consequently, for one fixed C,
    `LH_D(x)<=C D[1+x+log(1/D)]`.                        `(142.6)`

    Combining (142.3), (142.5), and (142.6), the assumption
    `D<exp(-3x)` would give
    `c_0x^2exp(-2x)<=CD[1+x+log(1/D)]
       <=C(1+4x)exp(-3x)`,
    because the last function is increasing in D on that interval.  This is
    impossible for large x.  Sharp curvature therefore forces the concrete
    lower bound
    `r(log p)>=p^(-3)` for every sufficiently large prime p.              `(142.7)`

    Bound (142.7) reopens the compensated local contact rather than proving
    curvature.  At `x=log p`, run the item-122 construction with its original
    U-width and oscillation `eta=exp(-10x)=p^(-10)`.  The outgoing derivative
    mass is negligible, and tightness of the integrable incoming weight
    allows all but an arbitrarily small fixed fraction of the positive
    derivative moment A to be carried by finitely many targets in one fixed
    compact interval.  Fix that fraction below the strict item-122 defect
    margin.  On the x-bump,
    `|r(z)-r(x)|<=|U(z)-U(x)|=O(eta)` and (142.7) give
    `r(z)>eta`; on the fixed target compact, positivity and continuity give
    a fixed positive lower radius.  Choose all auxiliary bumps inside these
    regions and set the function to zero elsewhere.

    The resulting item-122 function has `|df/dU|<=1` and total oscillation
    below eta.  It is therefore d-Lipschitz: the U branch controls every
    local pair, while any pair on which f differs and the hub branch is
    shorter has at least one active endpoint of radius greater than eta.
    A sufficiently close pair in the slope-one plateau at x has U-distance
    below eta and hub length at least `2r(x)-O(eta)`, so its d-distance is
    exactly its U-distance and f saturates it.  Item 122's fully compensated
    generator calculation is unchanged and gives
    `(Lf)'(x)+U'(x)/2=A+o(1)>0`, contradicting the necessary contact
    inequality.

    Hence no positive U-Lipschitz radius tending to zero on one tail can make
    this global line-plus-star metric contract at rate `1/2`.  The proof
    covers radii far below every ordinary scale as well as polynomial radii:
    the former are exposed by the discrete prime atom, and the atom-forced
    lower radius makes the latter accessible to the exact item-122 contact.
    It does not cover genuinely pair-dependent same-tail shortcuts which do
    not factor through a single hub radius.

143. **Prime-atom propagation closes every pair-dependent same-tail
    shortcut graph.**  Consider exactly the intrinsic graph class left after
    item 142: the line has path cost `d_U`, and for every prime power q one
    may add an undirected edge `z<->z+log q` of an arbitrary positive
    state/channel cost `a_q(z)`.  Let d be the resulting shortest-path
    metric and assume separation, `d<=d_U`, finite second moment, and the
    item-94 density hypothesis.  Suppose for contradiction that d contracts
    at rate `1/2`.

    Item 139 first gives a uniform fact on every fixed compact K:
    `b_K:=inf{a_q(z):{z,z+log q} intersects K}>0`.         `(143.1)`
    Otherwise a zero-cost sequence would have a compact endpoint.  If
    `I` is compactly contained in a slightly larger buffered K, then for all
    `s<min(b_K,dist_(d_U)(I,K^c))` and all `u,v in I` with
    `d_U(u,v)<s`, one has `d(u,v)=d_U(u,v)`.  Indeed a path using a shortcut
    before leaving K pays at least b_K, while a line path leaving K pays the
    buffer distance; the direct U-segment is therefore shortest.

    Use the item-122 contact at an arbitrary sufficiently large x with its
    original U-height and U-width
    `eta_x=exp(-10x)`.  Tightness of the positive incoming derivative moment
    permits a fixed fraction larger than the item-122 threshold (and in
    particular larger than one half after fixing the strict margin) to be
    carried by finitely many auxiliary prime-target bumps in one fixed
    compact I; the outgoing derivative tail is negligible.  The
    central x-bump is the only other support component.  All analytic and
    compensated-J estimates of item 122 are unchanged and give a strict
    contact defect.  Hence the function cannot be d-Lipschitz.  Its
    oscillation is below eta_x, so there must be a direct shortcut edge of
    assigned cost below eta_x with one endpoint t_x in the x-bump.  For x
    large, (143.1) excludes every compact target bump.  Moreover
    `d_U(t_x,x)<eta_x`.  Items 136 and 139 exclude, after increasing x, a
    blocking edge with a compact endpoint, an opposite-tail endpoint, or a
    uniform vanishing translation.  Thus both endpoints of the blocker lie
    in the positive tail.

    The required orientation of that edge follows from a quantitative
    prime-atom mismatch.  Let `u<v=u+log Q` be any positive-tail
    prime-power edge, put `D=d(u,v)`, and assume D is small.  Ordinary PNT
    supplies a prime `p in [exp(u),2exp(u)]` different from the prime base of
    Q.  Set `z=u-log p in [-log 2,0]`.  The incoming p-atom of the physical
    jump kernel Q_u lands exactly at z and has weight
    `w_(u,p)=[log p/sqrt p]K(z)/C(u)>=c u exp(-u)`.       `(143.2)`

    No prime atom of Q_v lands closer than `c exp(-v)` in ordinary
    coordinate to z.  An incoming atom there would have the form
    `v-log n`; exact equality would require `n=Qp`, which is not a prime
    power because p has a different base.  Otherwise integrality gives
    `|log(n/(Qp))|>=c/(Qp)>=c exp(-v)` whenever the target lies in a fixed
    enlargement of I.  By (143.1) and the buffered equality above, the same
    lower bound, after changing c, holds in d.  Take
    `s=c exp(-v)/3` and
    `phi=(s-d(.,z))_+`.  Then `phi(u)=phi(v)=0`.
    Positivity of all jump terms and (143.2) give
    `Lphi(u)>=w_(u,p)s>=c u exp(-u-v)`.                  `(143.3)`
    The d-ball contains no Q_v prime target.  On its compact U-coordinate
    realization the archimedean jump density from v is `O(exp(-v))`, so
    `Lphi(v)<=C exp(-v)s^2=O(exp(-3v))`.                 `(143.4)`

    Apply the two max-cone repairs of `+phi` and `-phi` at `(u,v)`.  Each is
    an exact contact and differs from its candidate by at most D.  The
    generator of such a correction is uniformly
    `O(D[1+log(1/D)])`.  For completeness, the local arch part at a tail
    point r is bounded by
    `[K(r-1)/C(r)] int_0^1 min{D,M_r t}dt/t`,
    `M_r=sup_(|h|<=1)U'(r+h)`.
    This is at most
    `C D[1+log(1/D)]`, because `K/C` and
    `(K/C)log M_r` are bounded on the positive half-line.  The nonlocal arch
    and total prime rates are uniformly bounded, so their correction is
    `O(D)`.  The two sharp contact inequalities, (143.3), and (143.4)
    therefore force the edge-mismatch bound
    `D log(1/D)>=c u exp(-u-v)`                          `(143.5)`
    for all sufficiently large u<v, after changing c.

    Return to the blocker at x.  Its intrinsic length is no larger than its
    assigned cost, hence below `eta_x`.  Since
    `D log(1/D)<=10x exp(-10x)`, (143.5) rules out a blocker whose larger
    endpoint is `t_x=x+o(1)`: for an inward edge its right side is at least
    `c exp(-2x+o(x))`.  Thus the blocker points outward.  If its farther
    endpoint is y, (143.5) gives
    `y>=9x-O(log x)`, and in particular `y>=8x` eventually.  Also
    `d(x,y)<=d_U(x,t_x)+a_Q(t_x)<2exp(-10x)`.            `(143.6)`

    Repeat the same item-122 construction at y.  It again has only the local
    tail bump and the protected fixed-compact target bumps, so the argument
    iterates.  Starting at any sufficiently large `x_0`, it produces
    `x_(j+1)>=8x_j` and
    `d(x_j,x_(j+1))<2exp(-10x_j)`.  Consequently
    `x_j->infinity` and, by the triangle inequality,
    `d(x_0,x_j)<=3exp(-10x_0)` for every j.              `(143.7)`
    Forbidden compact or opposite-tail blockers cannot interrupt this
    chain: an infinite sequence of them contradicts item 139, while the
    same two-cone estimates give a fixed compact-atom or reset-moment
    contradiction once their costs are below the displayed eta scale.

    Finally compare `x_0` directly with `x_j`.  For any bounded unit
    d-Lipschitz h supported in the protected compact I, one has
    `h(x_0)=h(x_j)=0`.  Cone-repairing `+h` and `-h` at this pair and using
    (143.7) and the preceding generator bound gives
    `|Lh(x_0)-Lh(x_j)|<=C x_0 exp(-10x_0)`.              `(143.8)`
    Weighted PNT gives `Lh(x_j)->int h d beta^+`.

    This convergence is quantitatively incompatible with one incoming atom
    at `x_0`.  Choose a prime `p_0 in [exp(x_0),2exp(x_0)]`, put
    `z_0=x_0-log p_0 in I`, and let
    `w_0=[log p_0/sqrt(p_0)]K(z_0)/C(x_0)
         >=c x_0exp(-x_0)`.
    For a sufficiently small fixed kappa take
    `h=(s-d(.,z_0))_+`, `s=kappa w_0`.  The compact local equality makes its
    ball a U-ball.  Since beta^+ has bounded density there,
    `int h d beta^+<=C s^2`, whereas the p_0 atom gives
    `Lh(x_0)>=w_0s`.  Choosing kappa below the reciprocal density constant
    yields
    `Lh(x_0)-int h d beta^+>=c w_0^2
       >=c x_0^2exp(-2x_0)`,                             `(143.9)`
    contradicting (143.8) for large `x_0`.

    Thus no separating intrinsic graph metric generated by the U-line and
    arbitrary positive state/channel prime-power shortcut costs has sharp
    rate-`1/2` contraction together with the item-94 density hypotheses.
    This theorem includes nonattained positive floors, state-independent
    zero floors, pair-dependent same-tail hierarchies, and non-star shortcut
    networks.  It does not address an arbitrary metric not realized by this
    intrinsic graph construction.

144. **An abstract positive-tail completion obeys a prime-atom access
    modulus; compact noncollapse and a quasi-star bound then close it.**
    Let d be any separating
    metric with `d<=d_U` satisfying the item-94 moment and density hypotheses.
    Suppose finite states `R_n->+infinity` converge in the d-completion to a
    point h and that the weighted-PNT tail generator limit from items 136 and
    142 holds uniformly for the bounded d-Lipschitz cone functions used
    below.  Put `r(z)=d(z,h)`.  This distance-to-boundary function is
    automatically unit d-Lipschitz.  Let `x_j->+infinity` be any access
    sequence with `D_j=r(x_j)->0`.  No star formula for d and no assumption
    that the whole positive tail converges to h is made.

    The exact finite-state cone argument still applies.  If phi is bounded,
    d-Lipschitz, vanishes at `x_j` and h, and obeys
    `r(z)>=D_j+|phi(z)|+delta` on its support, apply
    `max{+/-phi,d(x_j,R_n)-d(.,R_n)}` at `(x_j,R_n)` and then let n tend to
    infinity.  Both corrections converge to
    `H_j=(D_j-r)_+`, with `H_j(x_j)=0`, `H_j(h)=D_j`.  The two sharp contact
    inequalities give exactly
    `|Lphi(x_j)-int phi d beta^+|
       <=LH_j(x_j)-int H_j d beta^+<=LH_j(x_j)`.          `(144.1)`
    Thus (144.1) is a theorem about finite contacts approaching the
    completion boundary, not a postulated generator at h.  No properness or
    compact-support assertion is hidden here.  Indeed
    `|d(z,R_n)-r(z)|<=d(R_n,h)` uniformly in z, so the cone corrections
    converge uniformly.  At the fixed finite state `x_j`, finite Levy
    cutoffs pass to the limit by this uniform convergence; cutoff removal is
    uniform because all corrections are unit d-Lipschitz and
    `d<=d_U`, so the local singular term has the common integrable majorant
    `d_U(x_j,z)J(|z-x_j|)K(z)/C(x_j)`.  The prime and nonlocal rates are
    finite there.  At `R_n` the passage is exactly the uniform bounded-cone
    tail-limit hypothesis stated above, and beta convergence follows from
    uniform convergence and the finite mass of `beta^+`.

    Select by ordinary PNT a prime `p_j` with
    `log p_j=x_j+o(1)`, and set `z_j=x_j-log p_j->0`.  The physical jump law
    at `x_j` has an atom at `z_j` of mass
    `omega_j=[log p_j/sqrt(p_j)]K(z_j)/C(x_j)
              =(2K(0)+o(1))x_jexp(-x_j)`.                `(144.2)`
    Since `r(z_j)->r(0)>0`, the distance inequality
    `d(z,h)-d(x,h)<=d(z,x)` shows that a sufficiently small d-ball about
    `z_j` misses both `x_j` and h.  On this entire, possibly nonproper,
    d-ball, the unit d-Lipschitz property of r ensures the support condition
    above once `D_j+s` is small: throughout the ball,
    `r(z)>=r(z_j)-s`, while `|phi_j(z)|<=s`.  Mere `d<=d_U` does not bound
    the physical size of this d-ball;
    its direction is the opposite of the needed inclusion.  Instead use
    separation and non-atomicity.  The balls `B_d(z_j,s)` decrease to the
    singleton as `s->0`, so choose `s_j>0`, also small enough for the support
    condition, with
    `beta^+(B_d(z_j,s_j))<=omega_j/4`.  For
    `phi_j=(s_j-d(.,z_j))_+`, positivity of the other jump terms gives
    `Lphi_j(x_j)>=omega_j s_j`, while
    `int phi_j d beta^+<=omega_j s_j/4`.  Hence
    `Lphi_j(x_j)-int phi_j d beta^+>=3omega_j s_j/4`.     `(144.3)`

    The correction has the same universal generator bound as in item 142.
    It is bounded by `D_j`, is unit d-Lipschitz, and vanishes at `x_j`.
    Bounded prime and far-archimedean rates contribute `O(D_j)`.  Locally,
    `H_j(x_j+t)<=min{D_j,M_j|t|}` with
    `M_j=sup_(|t|<=1)U'(x_j+t)`; using `J(t)<1/|t|`,
    `K(x_j+t)<=K(x_j-1)`, and the theta tail gives
    `LH_j(x_j)<=C D_j[1+x_j+log(1/D_j)]`.                `(144.4)`
    Equations (144.1)--(144.4) give the unconditional implicit access bound
    `D_j[1+x_j+log(1/D_j)]>=c omega_j s_j`,               `(144.5)`
    where `s_j` is any d-ball scale satisfying the displayed beta-mass
    condition.  This is the strongest conclusion available from separation
    alone; no quantitative lower bound on `s_j` follows from `d<=d_U`.

    A concrete exponential floor follows under the exact compact
    noncollapse hypothesis
    `beta^+(B_d(z,s))<=B_K s`                             `(144.6)`
    for z in one fixed neighborhood of zero and all small s.  Taking
    `s_j=omega_j/(2B_K)` recovers the lower bound
    `c x_j^2exp(-2x_j)` in (144.3).  The comparison in item 142 then proves
    `d(x_j,h)>=exp(-3x_j)` for all large j.               `(144.7)`

    There is a complete conditional closure under the precise transverse
    hypothesis needed by the localization argument.  Suppose for one fixed
    `c_0>0`, on the positive-tail and fixed compact regions used below,
    `d(z,w)>=min{d_U(z,w),c_0[r(z)+r(w)]}`.              `(144.8)`
    A weaker assumption that (144.8) holds whenever one endpoint lies in the
    active supports is sufficient.  At `x=x_j`, item 122 uses
    `eta=exp(-10x)`, which is smaller than `c_0r(x)` by (144.7).  On the
    fixed compact, (144.8) implies (144.6): for s below the positive compact
    radius, a d-ball of radius s is contained in the corresponding U-ball.
    Its incoming
    derivative targets can again be confined to one fixed compact; r has a
    positive minimum there.  The U-bumps are therefore d-Lipschitz by
    (144.8), and on the close slope-one contact pair the lower bound and
    `d<=d_U` force `d=d_U`.  The unchanged defect
    `(Lf)'(x)+U'(x)/2=A+o(1)>0` contradicts sharp contraction.

    Hence no quasi-star completion end can survive.  The implicit modulus
    theorem (144.5) itself is unconditional within the stated contraction
    and tail-limit hypotheses, but the exponential floor (144.7) is not: an
    arbitrary d-ball in a compact set can be much larger than a U-ball.  Such
    compact transverse collapse, as well as non-graph pair-dependent
    same-radius collapse, lies outside (144.8) and item 143 and remains
    uncovered rather than being silently identified with a star metric.

145. **Every two-cut high-tail Gram determinant is eventually positive
    unconditionally; its boundary-scale limit is the max kernel.**  For
    `Y>=X` put `f_X=K 1_[X,infinity)` and define the real symmetric matrix
    `Gamma_(X,Y)=(Q_W(f_(X_i),f_(X_j)))_(i,j=0,1)`, where
    `(X_0,X_1)=(X,Y)`.  If `s_rho=rho-1/2`, the absolute zero-side argument
    of item 141 polarizes to
    `Q_W(f_X,f_Y)=sum_rho A_(s_rho)(X)A_(-s_rho)(Y)`.    `(145.1)`
    Symmetry follows either from the form or by replacing `s_rho` with
    `-s_rho` in the all-zero sum.

    Put `L=U(X)=-K'(X)/K(X)` and `M=U(Y)`.  Equations (141.5)--(141.6), at
    the two endpoints, give uniformly for every zero in the whole strip
    `A_s(X)A_(-s)(Y)=K(X)K(Y)exp[s(X-Y)]
       [(L-s)(M+s)]^(-1)[1+delta_(X,Y)(s)]`,             `(145.2)`
    `|delta_(X,Y)(s)|<=C(1/L+1/M)`.
    Moreover
    `sum_rho |(L-s_rho)(M+s_rho)|^(-1)=O(log L/L)`      `(145.3)`
    whenever `M/L` stays in a fixed compact subset of `(0,infinity)`.
    This is the same dyadic zero-count estimate as (141.8).  Thus all errors
    in (145.2) may be summed absolutely; no termwise fixed-zero asymptotic is
    being promoted to a uniform statement.

    First take the boundary scale `Y=X+tau/L`, with fixed `tau>0`.  The theta
    tail and one derivative of (141.4) give
    `M=L+2tau+O_tau(1/L)`,
    `K(Y)/K(X)=exp(-tau)[1+O_tau(1/L)]`.                 `(145.4)`
    The required scaled zero sum is also unconditional.  Uniformly for tau
    in a fixed compact subset of `[0,infinity)`,
    `sum_rho exp[-s_rho tau/L]/[(L-s_rho)(M+s_rho)]
       =exp(-tau)log[L/(2pi)]/(2L)+O_tau(1/L)`.          `(145.5)`
    Here is the complete off-line and limiting audit.  Write
    `s_rho=eta+i gamma`, with `|eta|<=1/2`.  Replacing eta by zero in the
    exponential and both denominators costs
    `O_tau(log L/L^2)` by (145.3) and
    `sum (L^2+gamma^2)^(-3/2)=O(log L/L^2)`.  The remaining sum is
    `2int_0^infinity cos(tau T/L)(L^2+T^2)^(-1)dN(T)`.
    The Riemann--von Mangoldt formula
    `N(T)=[T/(2pi)]log[T/(2pi)]-T/(2pi)+O(log(2+T))`
    and Stieltjes integration give
    `(pi L)^(-1)int_0^infinity
       cos(tau t)[log(L/(2pi))+log t](1+t^2)^(-1)dt
       +O_tau(log L/L^2)`.
    The log-t integral is finite, and
    `int_0^infinity cos(tau t)(1+t^2)^(-1)dt
       =(pi/2)exp(-tau)`, proving (145.5).  The remainder in the zero-count
    formula is integrated against a derivative of size `O_tau(L^(-3))` on
    `T=O(L)` and an integrable tail, which proves the displayed uniform
    error rather than merely formal weak convergence.

    Let `C_X=K(X)^2 log[L/(2pi)]/(2L)`.  The diagonal theorem (141.11),
    (145.2), (145.4), and (145.5) now yield
    `Gamma_00=C_X+O(K(X)^2 log L/L^2)`,
    `Gamma_11=exp(-2tau)C_X+O_tau(K(X)^2 log L/L^2)`,
    `Gamma_01=exp(-2tau)C_X+O_tau(K(X)^2/L)`.            `(145.6)`
    Consequently
    `det Gamma_(X,X+tau/L)
       =K(X)^4[log(L/(2pi))/(2L)]^2
          exp(-2tau)[1-exp(-2tau)]
          +O_tau(K(X)^4 log L/L^2)>0`                  `(145.7)`
    for every fixed `tau>0` and all sufficiently large X.  At tau zero the
    two vectors coincide and the determinant is identically zero.  More
    generally, for fixed nonnegative a and b the normalized leading kernel
    is
    `Q_W(f_(X+a/L),f_(X+b/L))/C_X
       ->exp[-(a+b)-|a-b|]=exp[-2max(a,b)]`.             `(145.8)`
    This is a positive max/Brownian covariance kernel arising solely from
    the high-zero density.

    Fixed positive separation has an even simpler leading determinant.  Let
    `Y=X+h`, where h is fixed and positive.  Then
    `M/L->c=exp(2h)>1`.  The diagonal terms still satisfy
    `Q_W(f_X)=K(X)^2{log[L/(2pi)]/(2L)+O(log L/L^2)}`
    and the analogous formula with `(X,L)` replaced by `(Y,M)`.  The cross
    zero sum in (145.2) is only `O_h(1/L)`.              `(145.9)`
    To justify the cancellation in (145.9), rather than infer it from a
    finite zero plot, use Landau's unconditional fixed-x formula.  For each
    fixed `x=exp h>1` and heights avoiding zero ordinates,
    `sum_(0<Im rho<=T)x^rho
       =-[T/(2pi)]Lambda(x)+O_x(log(2+T))`,              `(145.10)`
    where `Lambda(x)=log p` if x is the integral prime power `p^k` and is
    zero otherwise.  This follows from the standard rectangular contour for
    `-(zeta'/zeta)(s)x^s`; its hypotheses require neither RH nor a zero-
    density estimate.  The functional-equation symmetries give, for either
    sign of the ordinate,
    `sum x^(-s_rho)=O_h(1+T)`.
    Replacing eta only in the two denominators in (145.2) costs
    `O_h(log L/L^2)`.  Partial summation of the last cumulative bound against
    `[(L-iT)(M+iT)]^(-1)` costs `O_h(1/L)`: its derivative is `O(L^(-3))`
    up to height L and `O(T^(-3))` afterwards.  This proves (145.9),
    including the prime-power resonance case in (145.10).

    It follows that
    `det Gamma_(X,X+h)=K(X)^2K(X+h)^2{
       log[L/(2pi)]log[M/(2pi)]/(4LM)+O_h(1/L^2)}>0`    `(145.11)`
    for every fixed `h>0` and all sufficiently large X.  The logarithm-
    squared diagonal product dominates the at-most-order-one Landau
    resonance in the squared cross term.

    These determinants do not detect an off-line zero.  The only use of the
    real parts in (145.5) was the unconditional bound `|eta|<=1/2`, and
    Landau's formula in (145.10) also sums all zeros without RH.  Adding any
    finite symmetric off-line quartet to the zero-side sum changes the
    entries by lower-order `O(K(X_i)K(X_j)/L^2)` terms and leaves
    (145.7)--(145.11) unchanged.  Nor does pairwise determinant positivity
    imply full Weil positivity: the matrix with diagonal one and every
    off-diagonal entry `-3/5` has all `2 by 2` principal determinants
    positive, but its `3 by 3` eigenvalue in the constant direction is
    `-1/5`.  Requiring every finite cut Gram matrix to be positive, rather
    than the two-cut asymptotics proved here, is exactly the form-dense
    RH-equivalent condition audited at the end of item 141.  Thus neither a
    Stieltjes/Hankel upgrade nor an independent theta/prime positivity
    mechanism is supplied by the two-cut determinant.

146. **The divisor identity has an exact positive Gaussian flux, but the
    literal lowest-mode orthogonal Gram ansatz has infinite congestion and the
    wrong threshold domain.**  This tests the orbit/current mechanism by the
    required A--E chain.  **A (central lemma).**  A successful version would
    have to turn the orbit flux below into a linear current selector with the
    exact demand divergence and congestion at most one in the physical
    `E_K` conductances.  **B (chain to RH).**  By item 56 this would give a
    contraction `T` with `B_F=T B_E`, hence `E_K>=F_K`; item 51 then gives
    Weil positivity and RH.  **C (new structure).**  The following positive
    flux identity is exact.  **D (where the construction stops).**  The
    identity controls divergence but not the K-weighted channel Gram or its
    form domain.  **E (falsifiers).**  Fixed-separation congestion and the
    threshold radical give rigorous
    global counterexamples to the literal orthogonal realization.

    Recall from item 140 that
    `a(r)=2pi r^2(2pi r^2-3)exp(-pi r^2)` and
    `D(x)=sum_n Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)K(x-log n)`.  Put
    `b(r)=2pi r^3exp(-pi r^2)` and
    `f(r)=b(r)/sqrt(r)=2pi r^(5/2)exp(-pi r^2)`.  Direct
    differentiation gives
    `a(r)=-b'(r)` and, for `r=m exp(-x)`,
    `(partial_x-1/2)f(r)=sqrt(r)a(r)`.                  `(146.1)`
    For every prime power q dividing m define the strictly positive channel
    `j_(m,q)(x)=Lambda(q)f(m exp(-x))/(2sqrt(m))`
    ` =pi Lambda(q)m^2 exp(-5x/2-pi m^2exp(-2x))`.       `(146.2)`
    Using `sum_(q|m)Lambda(q)=log m` and (140.1) now gives
    `D(x)=sum_(m>=1)sum_(q|m)(partial_x-1/2)j_(m,q)(x)`.
                                                                  `(146.3)`
    This is not a formal rearrangement: on every compact x-interval the
    channels and their first derivatives are bounded by a polynomial in m
    times `exp(-c m^2)`, while the total divisor weight is `log m`.
    Therefore both sums converge uniformly and termwise differentiation in
    (146.3) is valid.  The constant `1/2` is exactly the lowest exponential
    mode in `J(t)=sum_(k>=0)exp[-(2k+1/2)t]`.

    The most literal global orthogonal-channel Gram ansatz is nevertheless
    incompatible with physical capacity.  Define
    `R_j(x,y)=sum_(m,q|m)j_(m,q)(x)j_(m,q)(y)`.          `(146.4)`
    Tensoring this Gram with the matching lowest constant mode gives the
    ansatz `exp(-|x-y|/2)R_j(x,y)`.  If it is allocated edgewise into the
    physical archimedean conductance, congestion at most one necessarily
    requires it not to exceed
    `J(|x-y|)K(x)K(y)`.  Fix h>0 and write `R=exp x`.  Keeping only q=2 and
    even m, the elementary Riemann-sum limit is
    `sum_(2|m)j_(m,2)(x)j_(m,2)(x+h)`
    ` ->3(log 2)^2exp(-5h/2)/[16(1+exp(-2h))^(5/2)]>0`. `(146.5)`
    Indeed the sum equals
    `pi^2(log 2)^2exp(-5h/2)R^(-5)
       sum_(2|m)m^4exp[-pi(1+exp(-2h))(m/R)^2]`,
    and `R^(-1)sum_(2|m)F(m/R)->(1/2)int_0^infinity F(t)dt`; evaluating the
    Gaussian fourth moment gives (146.5).  In contrast,
    `J(h)K(x)K(x+h)->0` double exponentially.  Consequently this ansatz's
    edgewise congestion ratio tends to infinity for every fixed h>0.
    Pointwise domination is not asserted to be necessary for an arbitrary
    signed or nonlocal Hilbert contraction.

    There is also a finite rigorous witness, so the conclusion does not rest
    on an asymptotic plot.  At `x=3`, `h=log 2`, the single channel
    `(m,q)=(16,2)` satisfies
    `exp(-h/2)j_(16,2)(x)j_(16,2)(x+h)
       >J(h)K(x)K(x+h)`.                                `(146.6)`
    The script bounds each K above by its n=1 theta term plus the explicit
    geometric tail obtained from
    `n^4<=exp(n^2)` and `(n+1)^2-n^2>=5` for n>=2.  At 220-bit Arb precision
    it encloses the two sides respectively near `9.8332959*10^(-4)` and
    below `3.174*10^(-2737)`; the interval comparison itself, not the printed
    decimals, is the certificate.

    The same current has the wrong natural form domain.  Let
    `g=(-K''+K/4)/K`, an item-52 threshold radical with finite `E_K+F_K`
    energy and exact equality `E_K(g)=F_K(g)`.  Its positive tail is
    `g(x)=-4pi^2exp(4x)+22pi exp(2x)+O(1)`, whereas
    `j_(2,2)(x)=4pi(log 2)exp(-5x/2)[1+o(1)]`.           `(146.7)`
    Hence, on every strip `y=x+h` with h in a fixed compact subinterval of
    `(0,infinity)`, the integrand in
    `C_j[u]=(1/2)iint exp(-|x-y|/2)R_j(x,y)
                         |u(x)-u(y)|^2 dxdy`             `(146.8)`
    is bounded below by a positive constant times `exp(3x)` for all large
    x, using only the `(2,2)` channel.  Therefore `C_j[g]=infinity`.  It
    cannot be dominated by `E_K`, nor can it be a positive residual square
    on the domain on which `E_K-F_K` must vanish.  No conclusion about the
    item-105 vertical cross is drawn: a compact perturbation can be
    channel-orthogonal, and the required overlap was not established.

    Faster constant modes do not repair these defects while retaining the
    exact arithmetic source.  More generally, for `f_p(r)=b(r)/r^p`,
    `(partial_x-p)f_p(mexp(-x))=(mexp(-x))^(1-p)a(mexp(-x))`. `(146.9)`
    Matching the `exp(-x/2)a/2` factor in D with x-independent divisor
    coefficients forces `p=1/2`; choosing `p=2k+1/2`, k>=1, changes the
    source.  Multiplication by K instead gives, with
    `J_0=sum_(m,q|m)j_(m,q)` and `U=-K'/K`,
    `KD=(KJ_0)'+(U-1/2)KJ_0`.                           `(146.10)`
    The fixed positive mode is then replaced by the unbounded variable
    killing U.  Constructing an additional global correction whose
    divergence and norm solve that equation is precisely the unrestricted
    Douglas selector in A, not a consequence of (146.3).

    Finally, no local Gaussian-current square can be substituted at the
    threshold.  Item 52 proves that every locally `L2`-closable analysis row
    in a residual SOS must annihilate all threshold radicals `u_p`; their
    local even-`L2` density then makes every row zero, contradicting strict
    small-support positivity.  Thus the exact identity (146.3) is a genuine
    new positive divergence formula, but (146.5)--(146.8) are a concrete
    death certificate for its literal positive orthogonal-current Gram
    realization.  No claim is made against a nonorthogonal, globally
    renormalized selector; proving that selector contractive remains exactly
    the RH-equivalent lemma in A.

    The deletion of a tempting bad-fiber comparison is part of the audit.
    The x in (146.1)--(146.3) is the physical coordinate, not item 53's
    modular fiber y.  Under the actual carrier pullback its positive-chart
    amplitude is
    `j^+_(m,q)(y)=Lambda(q)f(m^2exp(-y))/(2sqrt(m))`,
    while the negative-chart amplitude is
    `j^-_(m,q)(y)=Lambda(q)f(exp y)/(2sqrt(m))`.
    Therefore evaluating `j_(2,2)` at `y_0=log(8/3)` as though y were x was
    a coordinate collision and supplies no additional conductance.  The
    exact Cauchy--Schwarz/effective-capacity computation remains item 54's
    `G<V`; an uncosted flux amplitude cannot be added to G.

147. **Sharp contraction forbids compact-to-tail and opposite-tail collapse
    for every dominated metric, without an intrinsic-edge hypothesis.**
    Let d be an arbitrary separating metric on the real line with `d<=d_U`.
    Assume exactly the item-94 transport hypotheses: bounded d-Lipschitz
    functions are dense in `L2(pi)`, the d-second moment is finite, and the
    physical semigroup satisfies
    `W_d(P_t(x,.),P_t(y,.))<=exp(-t/2)d(x,y)`             `(147.1)`
    for every finite x,y and `t>=0`.  Then, for every ordinary compact set
    K, there are `epsilon_K>0` and `R_K<infinity` such that
    `d(a,b)>=epsilon_K` for `a in K`, `|b|>=R_K`.        `(147.2)`
    There are also `epsilon_opp>0,R_opp<infinity` such that
    `d(x,y)>=epsilon_opp` whenever `x<=-R_opp` and
    `y>=R_opp`.  No graph realization, assigned shortcut cost, star formula,
    properness, or compactness of d-balls is assumed.

    First exclude a compact-to-positive-tail sequence.  Otherwise take
    `A_n->a` in ordinary coordinate, `B_n->+infinity`, and
    `D_n=d(A_n,B_n)->0`.  Since `d<=d_U`, also `d(A_n,a)->0`.  Let h be any
    bounded unit d-Lipschitz function.  For `s in {+1,-1}` put
    `delta_(n,s)=D_n-[s h(B_n)-s h(A_n)]` and
    `G_(n,s)(z)=max{s h(z),s h(A_n)+D_n-d(z,B_n)}`.
    Then `0<=delta_(n,s)<=2D_n`, the function `G_(n,s)` is a bounded unit
    d-Lipschitz exact contact at `(A_n,B_n)`, and
    `0<=G_(n,s)-s h<=delta_(n,s)` everywhere.  Differentiate the full
    semigroup contraction (147.1) at this exact contact and identify its
    generator derivative by finite Levy cutoffs; this gives
    `L G_(n,s)(B_n)-L G_(n,s)(A_n)<=-D_n/2`.            `(147.3)`

    All cutoff and tail passages in (147.3) are properties of the physical
    generator already proved in item 139; they do not use its intrinsic-edge
    assumption.  Here are the bounds needed to make that independence
    explicit.  At the compact endpoint, the correction has sup norm at most
    `2D_n` and, on a fixed ordinary neighborhood, its increment is bounded
    by `min{L|t|,4D_n}` for one fixed L.  Since `J(t)<1/|t|`, its local Levy
    contribution is
    `O(D_n[1+log(1/D_n)])`; the nonlocal archimedean and prime parts are
    `O(D_n)`, and the prime series is locally uniform under its theta
    majorant.  Thus
    `L G_(n,s)(A_n)->s Lh(a)`.

    At the remote endpoint, the local archimedean bound is controlled by
    `C(B_n)^(-1)K(B_n-1)
       sup_(|t|<=1)U'(B_n+t)->0`; outside that window the sup-norm correction
    and theta tightness apply.  The weighted-PNT reset limit for the fixed
    bounded function h is valid because h is d-Lipschitz, hence U-Lipschitz,
    and the incoming measures are tight.  Since
    `|h(B_n)-h(A_n)|<=D_n` and `beta^+(R)=1/2`, this proves
    `L G_(n,s)(B_n)->s[int h d beta^+-(1/2)h(a)]`.       `(147.4)`
    These common estimates are uniform over the two cone signs and remove
    the Levy cutoff after the contact derivative; no generator at a
    completion point has been postulated.

    Letting n tend to infinity in (147.3), first for s=+1 and then for
    s=-1, forces the exact identity
    `Lh(a)=int h d beta^+-(1/2)h(a)`                    `(147.5)`
    for every bounded d-Lipschitz h.  This is impossible at a finite state.
    Indeed the physical jump law at a has a q=2 atom at
    `z_0=a+log 2` of mass
    `omega_2=[log 2/sqrt(2)]K(a+log 2)/C(a)>0`, whereas
    `beta^+` is nonatomic.  Separation gives `r_0=d(a,z_0)>0`.  For
    `0<rho<r_0/3`, the bounded test
    `h_rho(z)=(rho-d(z,z_0))_+` vanishes at a.  Positivity of all physical
    jump terms gives `Lh_rho(a)>=omega_2 rho`, while (147.5) gives
    `Lh_rho(a)=int h_rho d beta^+
       <=rho beta^+(B_d(z_0,rho))=o(rho)`.              `(147.6)`
    The last limit uses only finiteness and non-atomicity of `beta^+`: the
    decreasing d-balls have intersection `{z_0}`.  The ball need not be
    proper.  It also misses an ordinary neighborhood of a, by the triangle
    inequality and `d<=d_U`, so the Levy integral in (147.6) is legitimate.
    This contradiction excludes the positive tail.  Reflection, with the
    established `beta^-` reset law, excludes the negative tail.

    Compactness now upgrades sequential exclusion to the uniform statement
    (147.2).  If (147.2) failed, choose `a_n in K` and `|b_n|->infinity` with
    `d(a_n,b_n)->0`, then pass to an ordinarily convergent compact-endpoint
    subsequence and to one of the two tails, contradicting (147.6).

    The opposite-tail assertion is similar but leaves no finite endpoint.
    If `X_n->-infinity`, `Y_n->+infinity`, and `d(X_n,Y_n)->0`, apply the same
    two cone repairs to an arbitrary bounded unit d-Lipschitz h.  After a
    subsequence the two endpoint values have the same limit, and the two
    weighted-PNT reset formulas make the common holding term cancel.
    Equation (147.3) for both signs would therefore imply
    `int h d(beta^+-beta^-)=0`                           `(147.7)`
    for every bounded d-Lipschitz h.  But, with `pi=4CK dx`,
    `d(beta^+-beta^-)/d pi=-tanh(x/2)`, a nonzero bounded `L2(pi)` function.
    Density of the bounded d-Lipschitz class in item 94 makes (147.7)
    impossible.  A sequential contradiction again gives uniform
    `epsilon_opp,R_opp`.

    This theorem supplies an exact missing premise in item 144.  If
    `R_n->+infinity` converges in the d-completion to h, then for every
    ordinary compact K, (147.2) and passage to the completion limit give
    `inf_(a in K)d(a,h)>0`.                              `(147.8)`
    In particular `r(0)=d(0,h)>0`, and the same positive lower bound holds
    for the prime targets `z_j->0`.  Sufficiently small d-balls about those
    targets cannot contain remote physical states.  On the remaining fixed
    ordinary compact, `d<=d_U` and separation make the identity map a
    homeomorphism onto its d-image, so qualitative ball shrinkage is
    uniform.  Nothing here gives the linear measure bound
    `beta^+(B_d(z,rho))<=B rho` required in (144.6): the inverse compact
    modulus may be arbitrarily nonlinear.  Nor does (147.2) restrict pairs
    whose two endpoints both escape into the same tail.  Arbitrary
    nonintrinsic same-tail collapse, and its missing quantitative compact
    transverse modulus, therefore remain precisely outside this theorem.

148. **Every all-threshold Hilbert chord containing the complete derivative
    feature family fails sharp contraction.**  Put
    `F_0=(-D^2+1/4)K` and
    `phi_n(x)=F_0^(n)(x)/K(x)`, `n>=0`.  These are genuine operator
    eigenfunctions of the physical Markov generator:
    `T phi_n=(1/2)phi_n`.                              `(148.1)`
    Here is the full domain and moment check.  For `sigma=+/-1/2`, integration
    by parts and the double-exponential K tail give
    `int exp(sigma x)F_0^(n)(x)dx
       =(-sigma)^n(1/4-sigma^2)int exp(sigma x)K(x)dx=0`.
    Thus both the `C` and `S` moments of `F_0^(n)` vanish, and in particular
    `int phi_n dmu=0`.  Moreover
    `widehat(F_0^(n))(t)=(it)^n(t^2+1/4)widehat K(t)`,
    a nonzero constant multiple of
    `(it)^n(t^2+1/4)Xi(t)`, so `F_0^(n)` lies in the full Weil radical.
    Polarizing item 51 therefore gives, for every form-core u,
    `E_K(phi_n,u)=2c<phi_n,u>_mu=(1/2)<phi_n,u>_mu`.
    The theta derivative bounds give
    `|phi_n^(j)(x)|<=C_(n,j)exp(M_(n,j)|x|)` for fixed n,j.
    They put `phi_n` in `L2(mu)`, cancel the local
    `J(h)~1/(2|h|)` singularity, and dominate every prime shift by the
    double-exponential K tail.  Thus `phi_n` is in the closed form domain;
    the displayed weak identity and the representation theorem put it in
    `Dom(T)` and prove (148.1).  Even and odd n supply both parity sectors;
    no odd eigenfunction has been inferred from even moment orthogonality.

    The infinite feature can be made continuous and square integrable without
    dropping any coordinate.  Let
    `M_(n,j)=sup_(|x|<=j)|phi_n(x)|` and choose, for example,
    `a_n=2^(-n)/[1+||phi_n||_(L2(pi))
                    +max_(1<=j<=n)M_(n,j)]>0`,
    with the empty maximum omitted at n=0.  Then
    `Phi(x)=(a_n phi_n(x))_(n>=0)` is locally uniformly an `ell2`-valued
    continuous map and
    `int||Phi||^2dpi<infinity`.  Its mean is zero.  Consequently
    `d_Phi(x,y)=||Phi(x)-Phi(y)||_(ell2)` has finite pi-second moment.
    The next lemma also proves that Phi is injective.

    **Unique nonnegative secant lemma.**  Fix `x!=y`.  If
    `Phi(v)-Phi(u)=alpha[Phi(y)-Phi(x)]` with `alpha>=0`, then either
    `u=v` and `alpha=0`, or `(u,v)=(x,y)` and `alpha=1`.  Indeed every
    `a_n` is nonzero, so equality of all coordinates makes every derivative
    at zero vanish for
    `H(z)=F_0(v+z)/K(v)-F_0(u+z)/K(u)
          -alpha F_0(y+z)/K(y)+alpha F_0(x+z)/K(x)`.
    The common analytic strip of the theta series makes H vanish first near
    zero and then on the real line.  Fourier transformation gives
    `widehat F_0(t)[exp(itv)/K(v)-exp(itu)/K(u)
       -alpha exp(ity)/K(y)+alpha exp(itx)/K(x)]=0`.
    Since `widehat F_0=(t^2+1/4)widehat K` is nonzero on a real open set, the
    exponential polynomial in brackets vanishes identically.  Equivalently,
    `K(v)^(-1)delta_v-K(u)^(-1)delta_u
       =alpha[K(y)^(-1)delta_y-K(x)^(-1)delta_x]`.       `(148.2)`
    Positivity of K and uniqueness of the positive and negative parts of
    these two-atom measures give exactly the two alternatives.  Taking
    alpha zero also proves injectivity.

    This rigidity persists after adding features.  Let
    `Psi=(Phi,Theta)` take values in an orthogonal Hilbert direct sum, where
    every scalar coordinate of Theta is another mean-zero threshold
    eigenfunction and `int||Psi||^2dpi<infinity`; assume the resulting map is
    continuous so its chord cost is lower semicontinuous.  Projection of a
    nonnegative secant-ray identity for Psi onto the Phi coordinates gives
    the same identity for Phi with the same alpha.  Thus (148.2) still gives
    the unique-ray conclusion.  In particular, no further square-summable
    family of threshold coordinates can evade the lemma.

    Now suppose, toward a contradiction, that for
    `d(x,y)=||Psi(x)-Psi(y)||` the physical semigroup obeys
    `W_d(P_t(x,.),P_t(y,.))<=r d(x,y)`,
    `r=exp(-t/2)`, for one fixed `t>0` and all x,y.  On a common full-pi set,
    the vector form of (148.1) is
    `int Psi(z)P_t(x,dz)=r Psi(x)`.                      `(148.3)`
    The Bochner integral is legitimate by invariance and the finite second
    moment.  For distinct x,y in this set put
    `e=[Psi(y)-Psi(x)]/d(x,y)`.  The affine feature
    `f_e(z)=<e,Psi(z)>` is 1-Lipschitz and (148.3) gives the exact dual lower
    bound
    `int f_e d[P_t(y)-P_t(x)]=r d(x,y)`.
    Hence the assumed upper bound is equality.  A minimizing coupling gamma
    exists because the chord cost is lower semicontinuous and the marginals
    have finite first moment.  Equality in
    `<e,Psi(v)-Psi(u)><=||Psi(v)-Psi(u)||`
    forces
    `Psi(v)-Psi(u)=s e`, `s>=0`, for gamma-almost every `(u,v)`.
    The unique-secants lemma says that gamma is supported only on the
    diagonal and the single ordered pair `(x,y)`.  Its cost is `r d(x,y)`,
    so that pair has mass exactly r.  Therefore
    `P_t(x)-r delta_x=P_t(y)-r delta_y`                  `(148.4)`
    and the common remainder is a positive measure of mass `1-r`.

    Fixing one point shows that the remainder in (148.4) is independent of x
    on the full-pi set.  Invariance of pi then identifies its normalization
    with pi, and consequently
    `P_t=r I+(1-r)Pi` on `L2(pi)`.                       `(148.5)`
    Self-adjoint spectral calculus and injectivity of
    `lambda -> exp(-t lambda)` now force `T=1/2` on the whole centered
    `L2(pi)` space.

    The physical local singularity contradicts that conclusion directly.
    Choose `chi in C_c^infinity(R)` equal to one on `[-1,1]` and put
    `u_N(x)=chi(x)exp(iNx)`.  For `0<h<1/2` and
    `x in [-1/2,1/2]`, both cutoff values equal one.  If
    `k_0=min_(|z|<=1)K(z)>0`, then
    `J(h)>=exp(-1/4)/(2h)` and the archimedean part of the energy satisfies
    `E_K(u_N)>=c_0 int_(1/N)^(1/2)[1-cos(Nh)]dh/h
               >=c_0 log N-C_0`,                       `(148.6)`
    where `c_0>0`; the last bound follows by one integration by parts in
    `int_1^(N/2)cos(s)ds/s`.  All prime terms are nonnegative.  Subtracting
    the mu-mean leaves the energy unchanged, while the centered `L2(mu)`
    norm is bounded above uniformly in N.  Equation (148.5) would instead
    make that energy exactly one half of the squared norm.  This contradicts
    (148.6).

    Thus no chord built entirely from mean-zero threshold eigenfeatures and
    containing the complete derivative-radical subfamily can have all-pairs
    rate-`1/2` contraction.  The
    obstruction is a nonlinear Kantorovich equality constraint: affine
    secant contacts do have exact curvature `1/2`, but equality makes every
    optimal coupling live on the same secant ray, and the analytic feature
    curve has no other such ray.  This argument uses the full physical
    semigroup and the local J singularity; it does not infer transport from
    the threshold spectral identities or from finite-dimensional plots.

149. **Poisson renormalization removes every homogeneous q-lattice tail, but
    the literal unit-channel q-orthogonal realization retains logarithmically divergent
    transition congestion.**  Here is the required A--E search chain.
    **A (central lemma).**  One would need a signed Hilbert current whose
    divergence is exactly the divisor demand in (146.3) and whose norm is
    dominated, with constant one, by the physical `E_K` conductances.
    **B (chain to RH).**  Such a current gives the item-56 Douglas
    contraction `B_F=T B_E`, hence `E_K>=F_K`; item 51 then proves RH.
    **C (new structure).**  Jacobi--Poisson summation removes an exact
    homogeneous mode separately on every prime-power lattice while
    preserving its divergence.  **D (where it stops).**  The renormalized
    current is `ell2` but the scalar coordinate-sum divergence is unbounded,
    and the moving transition block has norm squared of order x.  **E
    (falsifiers).**  This defeats the literal unit-channel q-orthogonal raw or K-weighted
    edgewise allocation and every tested hard or smooth two-chart split.  It
    does not exclude an arbitrary nonorthogonal signed correlation.

    Let q run over prime powers, with `Lambda(q)=log p` for `q=p^k`, and
    group the item-146 channels by
    `J_q(x)=sum_(n>=1)j_(qn,q)(x)`.  Put `X=exp x` and `t=q/X`.  Then
    `J_q(x)=pi Lambda(q)q^2X^(-5/2)
                    sum_(n>=1)n^2exp(-pi t^2n^2)`.
    For `theta(t)=sum_(n in Z)exp(-pi t^2n^2)`, Jacobi's identity
    `theta(t)=t^(-1)theta(1/t)` and one differentiation give
    `sum_(n>=1)n^2exp(-pi t^2n^2)
       =1/(4pi t^3)+sum_(k>=1)[1/(2pi t^3)-k^2/t^5]
                                      exp(-pi k^2/t^2)`. `(149.1)`
    All series and their derivatives converge normally on compact subsets of
    `(0,infinity)`, so this differentiation is exact.  Consequently
    `J_q(x)=H_q(x)+Jtilde_q(x)`,
    `H_q(x)=Lambda(q)X^(1/2)/(4q)`,
    `Jtilde_q(x)=Lambda(q)X^(-1/2)F(q/X)`,              `(149.2)`
    where the two exactly equal formulas
    `F(t)=pi t^2sum_(n>=1)n^2exp(-pi t^2n^2)-1/(4t)`
    `    =[1/(2t)]sum_(k>=1)(1-2pi k^2/t^2)
                                      exp(-pi k^2/t^2)` `(149.3)`
    are useful on the two reciprocal scales.

    Since `(partial_x-1/2)H_q=0`, the q-lattice source is unchanged:
    `D_q:=(partial_x-1/2)J_q
          =(partial_x-1/2)Jtilde_q`,
    `D=sum_q D_q`.                                      `(149.4)`
    The last source series is absolutely convergent locally.  This follows
    either by regrouping the normally convergent `(m,q)` series in (146.3),
    using `sum_(q|m)Lambda(q)=log m`, or directly from the Gaussian bound on
    `(partial_x-1/2)J_q`.  In contrast, the scalar amplitude series is not
    convergent: for fixed X and q tending to infinity,
    `Jtilde_q=-Lambda(q)X^(1/2)/(4q)
       +O_X(Lambda(q)q^2exp(-pi q^2/X^2))`, and
    `sum_q Lambda(q)/q=infinity`.                        `(149.5)`
    Thus (149.4) is a coordinatewise differentiated identity, not the
    application of the bounded all-ones functional to an `ell2` vector.

    The renormalized vector itself is square summable.  From (149.3),
    `F(t)=-1/(4t)+O(t^2exp(-pi t^2))` as `t->infinity`, while
    `F(t)=-pi t^(-3)exp(-pi/t^2)[1+O(t^2)]`
    as `t->0`.                                          `(149.6)`
    Hence `F in L2(0,infinity)`, and splitting q at X proves
    `sum_q|Jtilde_q(x)|^2<infinity` for every x.  Equivalently, with
    `s=x-log q` and
    `A(s)=exp(-s/2)F(exp(-s))`,
    `Jtilde_q(x)=Lambda(q)q^(-1/2)A(x-log q)`,           `(149.7)`
    where A belongs to both `L1(R)` and `L2(R)`,
    `A(s)=-exp(s/2)/4+O(exp(-5s/2)exp(-pi exp(-2s)))` as
    `s->-infinity`, and A is double-exponentially small as
    `s->+infinity`.

    The exact size of the moving transition cannot be removed by this
    orthogonalization.  The only arithmetic input needed is the ordinary PNT
    with any classical zero-free-region error, for example
    `theta_p(Y):=sum_(p<=Y)log p
       =Y+O(Y exp[-c sqrt(log Y)])`.                    `(149.8)`
    Partial summation, with the contribution of `p^k`, k>=2, bounded by
    `O(sqrt(Y)log^2Y)`, gives
    `A_2(Y):=sum_(q<=Y)Lambda(q)^2
       =YlogY-Y+O(YlogY exp[-c sqrt(logY)]
                         +sqrt(Y)log^2Y)`.              `(149.9)`
    Apply Stieltjes partial summation to
    `X^(-1)int F(u/X)^2dA_2(u)`.  The bounds (149.6) give an integrable
    dyadic majorant at zero and infinity; (149.9) gives convergence on every
    fixed compact t-range.  Therefore, with
    `I_0=int_0^infinity F(t)^2dt in (0,infinity)`,
    `sum_q|Jtilde_q(x)|^2=x I_0+o(x)` as `x->infinity`. `(149.10)`
    This use of PNT is an averaged prime-square count and contains no
    square-root PNT error, zero-density estimate, or RH assumption.

    There is also a rational-margin transition lower bound independent of
    the limiting argument.  For `2<=t<=3`, every term
    `t^2exp(-pi t^2n^2)` decreases with t, and
    `4pi sum_(n>=1)n^2exp(-4pi n^2)<1/1000`             `(149.11)`
    (use `pi>3`, `4pi<13`, `a=exp(-12)<1/160000`, and
    `sum n^2a^n=a(1+a)/(1-a)^3`).  Since `1/(4t)>=1/12`,
    `F(t)<=1/1000-1/12=-247/3000`.                     `(149.12)`
    Keeping only primes `2X<=p<=3X` and applying (149.8)--(149.9) yields
    `sum_(2X<=p<=3X)|Jtilde_p(x)|^2
       >=(247/3000)^2 X^(-1)sum_(2X<=p<=3X)(log p)^2
       =(247/3000)^2[1+o(1)]x`.                        `(149.13)`
    Thus the obstruction is concentrated at `q asymp X`, not in the
    discarded homogeneous tail.

    Fixed-separation Grams retain the same mass.  For fixed real h define
    `G_h(x)=sum_q Jtilde_q(x)Jtilde_q(x+h)`.  The same Stieltjes argument,
    now with the integrable product
    `F(t)F(exp(-h)t)`, gives
    `G_h(x)=exp(-h/2)x I(h)+o(x)`, locally uniformly for h in compact sets,
    `I(h)=int_0^infinity F(t)F(exp(-h)t)dt`.             `(149.14)`
    Dominated convergence on a compact h-interval makes I continuous, and
    `I(0)=I_0>0`.  Hence there is `h_0>0` such that `I(h)>I_0/2` for every
    `|h|<h_0`.  In particular every sufficiently small fixed positive
    separation has a positive Gram growing linearly in x.

    This proves the precise congestion failure.  Tensoring the q-orthogonal
    Gram with the lowest constant mode gives the raw edgewise ansatz
    `C_raw(x,x+h)=exp(-h/2)G_h(x)`.  Its K-weighted repair is
    `C_K(x,x+h)=K(x)K(x+h)C_raw(x,x+h)`.                `(149.15)`
    At the current-amplitude level this multiplication changes the
    first-order divergence.  With `U=-K'/K`, exactly
    `(partial_x+U-1/2)[K Jtilde_q]=K D_q`, whereas
    `(partial_x-1/2)[K Jtilde_q]=K D_q-U K Jtilde_q`.
    Thus `C_K` is being tested as the most favorable K-weighted norm repair,
    not asserted to be an already constructed constant-mode selector.
    The physical archimedean conductance is
    `C_E(x,x+h)=J(h)K(x)K(x+h)`, with
    `J(h)=exp(-h/2)/(1-exp(-2h))`.  For every fixed
    `0<h<h_0`, (149.14) gives
    `C_K/C_E=(1-exp(-2h))G_h(x)->infinity`,              `(149.16)`
    linearly in x; the raw ratio has the additional factor
    `[K(x)K(x+h)]^(-1)`.  Thus neither literal unit-channel q-orthogonal edgewise
    realization has congestion at most one.  This conclusion is not being
    extended to a signed nonlocal Douglas contraction, for which pointwise
    edge domination need not be necessary.

    The domain audit separates the two failures.  For the exact threshold
    radical `g=(-K''+K/4)/K`, item 105 gives
    `|g(x+h)-g(x)|^2>=c_h exp(8x)` for every fixed h>0 and large x.
    Equations (149.14)--(149.15) show that the raw orthogonal edge form has
    an infinite positive contribution on every compact h-subinterval of
    `(0,h_0)`.  Multiplication by K repairs this particular domain defect:
    (149.6)--(149.10) give
    `N(x):=sum_q|Jtilde_q(x)|^2<=C(1+x_+)` for `x>=0`
    and `N(x)<=Cexp(x)` for `x<=0`; Cauchy--Schwarz and the double-theta
    tail then make
    `iint exp(-|x-y|/2)K(x)K(y)sqrt[N(x)N(y)]
                  |g(x)-g(y)|^2dxdy<infinity`.          `(149.17)`
    But (149.16) proves that this K-weighted form still cannot be
    E-dominated.  The fixed-q Poisson residual is double-exponentially small
    in the positive tail, so item 146's former fixed-channel divergence no
    longer applies; no compensating item-105 vertical cross has been
    constructed.  Items 105 and 107 therefore remain an unmet independent
    label-domain gate rather than a claimed consequence of this calculation.

    Finally, an adaptive chart split cannot localize away the transition for
    free.  If chi is a smooth cutoff in `s=x-log q`, then
    `(partial_x-1/2)[J_q-H_q chi(s)]
       =D_q-H_q chi'(s)`.                               `(149.18)`
    A hard cutoff has the corresponding atom at `x=log q`, of magnitude
    `Lambda(q)/(4sqrt(q))` with sign fixed by the chosen orientation.  This
    prime-line boundary source is absent from (149.4).  The direct
    complementary gauge chart `-H_q[1-chi(s)]` has the opposite source and
    cancels it, but its algebraic sum with `J_q-H_qchi(s)` is exactly
    `Jtilde_q` from (149.2).

    The fixed-separation bridge for this two-chart test is also exact.  Take
    a smooth chi equal to zero near negative infinity and one near positive
    infinity (or take the hard step), and put
    `B(s)=exp(s/2)/4`, with A as in (149.7).  After factoring
    `Lambda(q)/sqrt(q)`, the two chart profiles are
    `a(s)=A(s)+B(s)[1-chi(s)]`,
    `b(s)=-B(s)[1-chi(s)]`, and `a+b=A`.                `(149.19)`
    Write `atilde_q(x)=Lambda(q)q^(-1/2)a(x-logq)` and
    `btilde_q(x)=Lambda(q)q^(-1/2)b(x-logq)`.
    Both a and b belong to `L2(R)`: at negative infinity the homogeneous
    terms in a cancel to the original Gaussian lattice profile, while b is
    `O(exp(s/2))`; at positive infinity chi is one and A is
    double-exponentially small.  The same holds for the hard step.  If the
    charts are orthogonal, (149.8)--(149.9), in the logarithmic q-coordinate,
    give locally uniformly for fixed h
    `sum_q[atilde_q(x)atilde_q(x+h)+btilde_q(x)btilde_q(x+h)]
       =x C_chi(h)+o(x)`,
    `C_chi(h)=int_R[a(s)a(s+h)+b(s)b(s+h)]ds`.           `(149.20)`
    Translation continuity in `L2` yields
    `C_chi(h)->||a||_2^2+||b||_2^2
       >=(1/2)||A||_2^2=I_0/2` as `h->0`.               `(149.21)`
    Hence every fixed tested cutoff retains a positive order-x
    fixed-separation Gram for all sufficiently small h>0, and its raw and
    K-weighted chart-orthogonal capacity ratios fail exactly as in
    (149.15)--(149.16).
    Thus the tested hard and smooth `q lessgtr exp x` splits do not produce a
    K-weighted exact-divergence selector.

    A genuinely nonorthogonal construction remains logically open, but its
    burden is now quantitative.  Fix
    `0<h<min(h_0,log(3/2))`.  On
    `2exp(h)X<=q<=3X`, both arguments q/X and q/[exp(h)X] lie in `[2,3]`;
    (149.12) and PNT give a positive diagonal cross mass `asymp x`.
    Negative off-diagonal correlations must cancel it down to `O(1)` while
    retaining all q-resolved sources.  The construction must also place the
    `ell2` current in the domain of a closed
    divergence operator even though the all-ones amplitude sum diverges by
    (149.5), prove the item-105 vertical cross and form domain, and satisfy
    the exact norm-one inequality in A.  Supplying an unrestricted operator
    with precisely those properties is the full Weil/Douglas factorization;
    no such operator is inferred from Poisson summation alone.

    **Independent audit.**  A separate reconstruction checked (149.1)--(149.21),
    including the `Lambda(q)` remainder in (149.5), the PNT normalization,
    fixed-separation constants, the threshold form-domain estimate, and the
    `L2` two-chart translation bridge.  It returned PASS; the unrestricted
    nonorthogonal signed correlation is explicitly not covered.

150. **The `2/5` snowflaked U metric has an exact three-cone contraction
    countercontact.**  This route had the following A--E structure.  **A:**
    for `d_alpha(x,y)=|U(x)-U(y)|^alpha`, prove sharp all-pairs contraction at
    rate `1/2`.  **B:** for every fixed `0<alpha<1`, concavity makes d_alpha a
    separating metric, its second moment is finite under pi, and bounded
    d_alpha-Lipschitz functions are dense (pull back compactly supported
    Lipschitz functions through the increasing bijection U).  Thus item 94
    would give the item-57 gap and hence RH.  **C:** the intended new
    structure was the sublinear snowflake suppression of short U-increments.
    **D:** existing path-curvature calculations do not optimize the resulting
    nonlocal folded contacts.  **E:** the exact contact below falsifies A for
    `alpha=2/5`.  No other exponent is inferred.

    Put
    `a=3log(2)/4`, `b=a+3/100`, `z=b-log(2)`,
    `D=[U(b)-U(a)]^(2/5)`, and `v=7/5`.  Define
    `f(x)=max{-|U(x)-U(a)|^(2/5),
               D-|U(x)-U(b)|^(2/5),
               v-|U(x)-U(z)|^(2/5)}`.                 `(150.1)`
    The distance-to-a-point functions are unit d_(2/5)-Lipschitz and a
    maximum preserves that property.  Direct interval evaluation proves
    `v<d_(2/5)(z,a)` and `|v-D|<d_(2/5)(z,b)`, so the three assigned cone
    heights are consistent.  Hence
    `f(a)=0`, `f(b)=D`, and (150.1) is a global exact contact.  In particular,
    this is not a finite-grid extension or an extrapolation from sampled
    Lipschitz constraints.

    For the item-57 generator the necessary derivative of sharp contraction
    at any bounded contact is
    `Lf(b)-Lf(a)+D/2<=0`.                               `(150.2)`
    The certificate evaluates the opposite sign with a strict rational
    margin:
    `Lf(b)-Lf(a)+D/2>9/100`.                            `(150.3)`
    It uses 180-bit Arb intervals.  Seven exact theta terms are followed by
    explicit geometric bounds for `K,K',K''`.  On `[-2,2]`, each of the four
    one-sided archimedean integrals uses `h=t^5`, which changes the local
    bound `J(h)|U(x+h)-U(x)|^(2/5)` into a regular integrand; 5000 interval
    cells cover each side.  The omitted central pieces are bounded from
    `J(h)<=exp(3h/2)/(2h)` and an interval upper bound for U'.  For `|x|>=2`,
    the certified majorants
    `K(x)<=4pi^2 exp(9|x|/2-pi exp(2|x|))` and
    `|K'(x)|<=16pi^3 exp(13|x|/2-pi exp(2|x|))`
    give the complete spatial tail.  Prime powers through 16 are evaluated
    individually; every omitted prime power is overcounted by all integers
    `n>=17`, using `Lambda(n)<=log n` and a geometric Gaussian tail.  The
    interval output encloses
    `U(a)=12.8653450374165...`, `U(b)=13.9924010735383...`, and
    `D=1.04900655911476...`; the comparison in (150.3), not these printed
    decimals, is the certificate.

    Although f is unbounded below, this causes no generator-domain shortcut.
    Let `f_M=max{f,-M}`.  For every sufficiently large M it is a bounded unit
    d_(2/5)-Lipschitz function with the same contact values at a and b.  The
    same estimates used in the certificate dominate each local endpoint
    integrand by
    `J(|x-r|)K(x)|U(x)-U(r)|^(2/5)`, which is integrable because `2/5>0`.
    Away from r the Gaussian bound
    `J(|x-r|)K(x)[1+|U(x)|^(2/5)]` is integrable, and the prime analogue is
    summable.  Dominated convergence gives
    `Lf_M(r)->Lf(r)` for `r=a,b`.  Thus (150.3) makes (150.2) fail for an
    actual bounded contact once M is large.  The finite-Levy derivative and
    cutoff removal use this same majorant, so no pointwise generator has been
    assumed outside its domain.

    The circularity audit is empty: only the theta series for K, elementary
    concavity, finite prime-power enumeration, and explicit exponential
    majorants are used.  There is no PNT error term, zero statement,
    square-root cancellation, Weil positivity, or finite-to-infinite
    positivity step.  The computation is a finite interval certificate for
    a single full-generator inequality, and that inequality alone is a valid
    infinitesimal falsifier of contraction.  Reproduce it with
    `PYTHONPATH=/tmp/pvdeps python3
       scripts/snowflake_three_cone_certificate.py`.

    **Independent audit.**  A separate reconstruction reran the certificate,
    checked the cone interpolation, all theta and omitted-tail bounds, the
    `h=t^5` Levy regularization, prime-power enumeration, bounded truncation,
    and the derivative-of-contraction orientation.  It returned PASS with the
    enclosed defect interval
    `[0.0922273244190384,0.117790121248074]`.

151. **The renormalized profile has an exact Xi multiplier, while ordered
    incidence cancels its transition congestion only through a nonclosable
    divergence.**  The A--E chain is as follows.  **A (central lemma).**  A
    surviving current proof still needs a Hilbert current and a closed
    physical divergence, on a domain containing the finite-label compact-x
    core, whose exact source is `sum_q D_q` and whose norm is dominated with
    constant one by the physical `E_K` conductances.  **B (chain to RH).**
    This is precisely the item-56 Douglas contraction; it gives `E_K>=F_K`,
    and item 51 then gives RH.  **C (new structure).**  The exact Mellin
    transform below identifies every bounded scale-stationary filter, while
    the ordered prime-power incidence vectors are an explicit high-pass in
    the label coordinate.  **D (where it stops).**  Stationary filters cannot
    cancel the small fixed-separation leading Gram without vanishing, and the
    incidence high-pass does cancel that Gram but has no closable scalar
    divergence.  **E (falsifier).**  A telescoping finite-label tensor tends
    to zero while its proposed divergence is fixed.  The conclusion is
    deliberately restricted to bounded scale-stationary filters and this
    ordered-incidence label readout.  It does not exclude a nonstationary closed
    divergence that couples x and q essentially.

    Retain `F` and `A` from (149.3) and (149.7), and write
    `theta(t)=sum_(n in Z)exp(-pi t^2 n^2)`.  The reciprocal-theta remainder
    `E(t):=[theta(t)-t^(-1)]/2
          =t^(-1)sum_(k>=1)exp(-pi k^2/t^2)`            `(151.1)`
    satisfies
    `F(t)=-(t/2)E'(t)`.                                 `(151.2)`
    At zero E is a Gaussian times a power; at infinity
    `E(t)=1/2-1/(2t)+O(exp(-pi t^2))`.  Thus
    `M_F(z):=int_0^infinity F(t)t^(z-1)dt` converges and is analytic for
    `Re z<1`.  On the genuine common absolute-convergence half-plane
    `Re z<0`, integration by parts in (151.2), followed by (151.1), gives
    `M_F(z)=(z/2)int_0^infinity E(t)t^(z-1)dt`
    `      =(z/4)pi^((z-1)/2)Gamma((1-z)/2)zeta(1-z)`
    `      =xi(z)/[2(z-1)]`.                            `(151.3)`
    The boundary term is zero because `t^zE(t)` vanishes at both ends on
    this half-plane.  The termwise integral is absolutely summable there,
    since it contains `sum k^(Re z-1)`.  Both sides of (151.3) are analytic
    on `Re z<1`; the last equality uses the standard functional equation for
    the completed zeta function.  The identity theorem therefore extends
    (151.3) throughout the full convergence half-plane.  In particular, for
    the Fourier convention `Ahat(gamma)=int_R A(s)exp(-i gamma s)ds`, the
    substitution `t=exp(-s)` gives the exact formula
    `Ahat(gamma)=M_F(1/2+i gamma)
       =xi(1/2+i gamma)/[2(-1/2+i gamma)]`.              `(151.4)`
    The right side is nonzero for almost every real gamma: Xi is a
    nonzero entire function, so its real zero set is discrete.  No assertion
    about where its other zeros lie is used.

    Formula (151.4) closes the regular scale-stationary cancellation idea.
    Let H be a Hilbert space and let S be a bounded translation-invariant
    map from `L2(R)` to `L2(R;H)`.  The standard Fourier multiplier theorem
    writes `(Sf)^hat(gamma)=m(gamma)fhat(gamma)` for an essentially bounded
    H-valued multiplier m.  Put `B=SA`.  Restrict attention to filters for
    which B and its translates have the same integrable dyadic majorants as
    in the PNT/Stieltjes argument of (149.14); compactly supported or
    sufficiently decaying `L1` convolution kernels are examples.  Then the
    filtered prime-power Gram has, for every fixed small h,
    `sum_q Lambda(q)^2/q
       <B(x-log q),B(x+h-log q)>_H
       =x R_B(h)+o(x)`,                                 `(151.5)`
    where `R_B(h)=int_R <B(s),B(s+h)>_H ds`.  If a proposed filter makes the
    left side `O(1)` for every sufficiently small fixed positive h, (151.5)
    forces `R_B(h)=0` on that interval.  Strong continuity of translation in
    `L2` gives `R_B(h)->||B||_2^2` as `h->0`, hence B=0.  Now
    `m(gamma)Ahat(gamma)=0` almost everywhere; (151.4) forces m=0 almost
    everywhere.  Thus S itself is zero.  This is not a claim about
    nonstationary x-dependent filters, nor about filters lacking the stated
    PNT-sampling majorant.

    There is nevertheless an exact label high-pass that removes the leading
    congestion.  Order the prime powers increasingly as `q_1<q_2<...`, let
    `(e_i)` be the standard basis of `ell2`, and put
    `a_i(x)=Jtilde_(q_i)(x)`, `v_i=e_i-e_(i+1)`, and
    `W(x)=sum_(i>=1)a_i(x)v_i`.                          `(151.6)`
    Since `a_i(x)->0`, telescoping gives the exact norm identity
    `||W(x)||_2^2=|a_1(x)|^2
                    +sum_(i>=2)|a_i(x)-a_(i-1)(x)|^2`. `(151.7)`
    To estimate it, set `X=exp x` and introduce the smooth reference
    `phi_X(u)=X^(-1/2)(log u)F(u/X)`.  At a prime, `a_q=phi_X(q)`.  At a
    proper prime power `q=p^k`, the discrepancy is exactly
    `r_q:=a_q-phi_X(q)
       =-(1-1/k)X^(-1/2)(log q)F(q/X)`.                 `(151.8)`
    Bertrand's theorem gives `q_(i+1)-q_i<q_i`.  Therefore Cauchy--Schwarz
    on every consecutive interval yields
    `sum_i|phi_X(q_(i+1))-phi_X(q_i)|^2
       <=int_2^infinity u|phi_X'(u)|^2du
       =O((1+x^2)/X)`.                                 `(151.9)`
    The final equality follows after `u=Xt` from (149.6) and the corresponding
    differentiated Gaussian bounds; the required integrals of F, F', and
    their logarithmic weights converge at both endpoints.

    The elementary count of proper prime powers up to Y is
    `O(sqrt(Y)log Y)`.  Splitting (151.8) at X and then into dyadic blocks,
    using boundedness of F on `(0,1]` and `F(t)=O(1/t)` for `t>=1`, gives
    `sum_(q=p^k,k>=2)|r_q|^2=O(x^3/sqrt X)`.            `(151.10)`
    Indeed the block `[2^jX,2^(j+1)X]` contributes at most a constant times
    `(x+j)^3 2^(-3j/2)/sqrt X`, and the block below X contributes
    `O(x^3/sqrt X)`.  The incidence map
    `(c_i)->sum_i c_i(e_i-e_(i+1))` has norm at most two.  Combining
    (151.7)--(151.10), including the double-exponentially small first term,
    proves
    `||W(x)||_2^2=O(x^3/sqrt X)=O(x^3exp(-x/2))->0`.    `(151.11)`
    Hence every fixed-h Gram `<W(x),W(x+h)>` also tends to zero by
    Cauchy--Schwarz.  Thus this construction genuinely defeats the moving
    transition-capacity obstruction of item 149; it is not being rejected
    by finite-dimensional extrapolation or by a residual positive Gram.

    Its fatal defect is instead exact nonclosability.  On the finite
    sequences define the scalar functional `ell(e_i)=-i`; then
    `ell(v_i)=1`.  Formally, and exactly for every finite truncation,
    `ell[(partial_x-1/2)W]=sum_i D_(q_i)`,               `(151.12)`
    whose infinite source limit is locally absolutely convergent by (149.4).
    But with
    `z_N=N^(-1)sum_(i=1)^N v_i
         =(e_1-e_(N+1))/N`,                            `(151.13)`
